The four operations on perverse motives
Florian Ivorra, Sophie Morel

Abstract
. Let k k be a field of characteristic zero with a fixed embedding σ : k ↪ 𝐂 \sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C} into the field of complex numbers. Given a k k -variety X X , we use the triangulated category of étale motives with rational coefficients on X X to construct an abelian category ℳ ⁡ ( X ) \mathscr{M}(X) of perverse mixed motives. We show that over Spec ( k ) \mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}(k) the category obtained is canonically equivalent to the usual category of Nori motives and that the derived categories D b ​ ( ℳ ​ ( X ) ) \mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X)) are equipped with the four operations of Grothendieck (for morphisms of quasi-projective k k -varieties) as well as nearby and vanishing cycles functors and a formalism of weights. In particular, as an application, we show that many classical constructions done with perverse sheaves, such as intersection cohomology groups or Leray spectral sequences, are motivic and therefore compatible with Hodge theory. This recovers and strengthens work by Zucker, Saito, Arapura and de Cataldo–Migliorini and provide an arithmetic proof of the pureness of intersection cohomology with coefficients in a geometric variation of Hodge structures.



Mathematics Subject Classification
14F42, 14F43, 18G55

Introduction

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero with a fixed embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}
into the field of complex numbers. A
k
-variety is a separated
k
-scheme of finite type. Unless otherwise specified, we will only consider quasi-projective
k
-varieties.

In the present work, we construct the four operations on the derived categories of perverse Nori motives. In order to combine the tools provided by Ayoub in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
and Beilinson
in
[
Bei87a
,
Bei87c
]
in a most efficient way, we define the abelian category of perverse Nori motives on a given
k
-variety as a byproduct of the triangulated category of constructible étale motives on the same variety. Over the base field the category obtained still coincides with the usual category of Nori motives but now, as we show, it is possible to equip the derived categories of these abelian categories with the four operations of Grothendieck as well as nearby and vanishing cycles functors. However, we leave the construction of the tensor product and internal
\mathrm{Hom}
operations on these categories to a later paper.

In particular, as an application, we show that many classical constructions done with perverse sheaves, such as intersection cohomology groups or Leray spectral sequences, are motivic and therefore compatible with Hodge theory. This recovers and strengthens works by Zucker
[
Zuc79
]
, Saito
[
Sai90b
]
, Arapura
[
Ara05
]
and de Cataldo–Migliorini
[
dCM10
]
. Moreover it provides an arithmetic proof via reduction to positive characteristic and the Weil conjectures of the pureness of the Hodge structure on intersection cohomology with coefficients in a geometric variation of Hodge structures.

Conjectural picture and some earlier works

Before going into more detail about the content of this paper, let us discuss perverse motives from the perspective of perverse sheaves and recall parts of the conjectural picture and related earlier works.

For someone interested in perverse sheaves, perverse motives can be thought of as perverse sheaves of geometric origin. However, the classical definition of these perverse sheaves as a full subcategory of the category of all perverse sheaves is not entirely satisfactory. Indeed, this category contains too many morphisms and consequently, as we take kernels and cokernels of morphisms which shouldn’t be considered, too many objects. For example, perverse sheaves of geometric origin should define mixed Hodge modules and therefore any morphism between them should also be a morphism of mixed Hodge modules. Therefore, one expects the category of perverse motives/perverse sheaves of geometric origin to be an abelian category endowed with a faithful – but not full – exact functor into the category of perverse sheaves.

According to Grothendieck, there should exist a
\mathbf{Q}
-linear abelian category
\mathbf{MM}(k)
whose objects are called mixed motives. Given an embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}
, the category
\mathbf{MM}(k)
should come with a faithful exact functor

\mathbf{MM}(k)\rightarrow\mathbf{MHS}

to the category of (polarizable) mixed
\mathbf{Q}
-Hodge structures
\mathbf{MHS}
, called the realization functor. The mixed Hodge structure on the
i
-th Betti cohomology group
H^{i}(X)
of a given
k
-variety
X
should come via the realization functor from a mixed motive
H_{\mathbf{M}}^{i}(X)
.
The appealing beauty of this picture lies in the expected properties of this category, in particular, the conjectural relations between extension groups and algebraic cycles (see e.g. Jannsen’s
[
Jan94
]
), or the relation with period rings and motivic Galois groups (see e.g. for a survey Ayoub’s
[
Ayo14d
]
).

As part of Grothendieck’s more general cohomological program, the category
\mathbf{MM}(k)
should underlie a system of coefficients. For any
k
-variety
X
, there should exist an abelian category
\mathbf{MM}(X)
of mixed motives along with a realization functor into the category of mixed Hodge modules (or simply of sheaves of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces) on the associated analytic space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
,
and their derived categories should satisfy a formalism of (adjoint) triangulated functors

\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{MM}(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{{\mathbf{M}}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{MM}(Y))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{{\mathbf{M}}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathbf{M}}^{!}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{MM}(X)),}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}^{{\mathbf{M}}}}

a formalism which has been at the heart of Grothendieck’s approach to every cohomology theory. Then, for a
k
-variety
a:X\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}k
,
the motive
H^{i}_{\mathbf{M}}(X)
would be given as the
i
-th cohomology of the image under
a^{\mathbf{M}}_{*}
of a complex of mixed motives
\mathbf{Q}^{\mathbf{M}}_{X}
that should realize to the standard constant sheaf
\mathbf{Q}_{X}
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
.
Grothendieck was looking for abelian categories modeled after the categories of constructible sheaves, but as pointed out by Beĭlinson and Deligne one could/should also look for categories modeled after perverse sheaves (see e.g. Deligne’s
[
Del94
]
).

Many attempts have been made to carry out at least partially but unconditionally Grothendieck’s program.

The most successful attempt in constructing the triangulated category of mixed motives (that is, conjecturally, the derived category of
\mathbf{MM}(X)
) stems from Morel–Voevodsky’s stable homotopy theory of schemes. The best candidate so far is the triangulated category
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
of constructible étale motivic sheaves (with rational coefficients) extensively studied by Ayoub in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
,
Ayo14a
]
. The theory developed in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
provides these categories with the Grothendieck four operations and, as shown by Voevodsky in
[
Voe02
]
, Chow groups of smooth algebraic
k
-varieties can be computed as extension groups in the category
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(k)
.

On the abelian side, Nori has constructed a candidate for the abelian category of mixed motives over
k
. The construction of Nori’s abelian category
\mathbf{HM}(k)
is tannakian in essence and, since it is a category of comodules over some Hopf algebra, it comes with a built-in motivic Galois group. Moreover any Nori motive has a canonical weight filtration and Arapura has shown in
[
Ara13
, Theorem 6.4.1]
that the full subcategory of pure motives coincides with the semi-simple abelian category defined by André in
[
And96
]
using motivated algebraic cycles (see also Theorem 10.2.5 in the book
[
HMS17
]

of Huber and Müller–Stach).
More generally, attempts have been made to define Nori motives over
k
-varieties. Arapura has defined a constructible variant in
[
Ara13
]
and the first author a perverse variant in
[
Ivo17
]
. However, the Grothendieck four operations have not been constructed (at least in their full extent) in those contexts. For example in
[
Ara13
]
, the direct image functor is only available for structural morphisms or projective morphisms and no extraordinary inverse image is defined.

Note that the two different attempts should not be unrelated. One expects the triangulated category
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
to possess a special
t
-structure (called the motivic
t
-structure) whose heart should be the abelian category of mixed motives. This is a very deep conjecture, even for
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}k
, which implies for example the Lefschetz and Künneth type standard conjectures (see Beilinsons’s
[
Bei12
]
). As of now, the extension groups in Nori’s abelian category of mixed motives are known to be related with algebraic cycles only very poorly.

However, striking unconditional relations between the two different approaches have still been obtained. In particular, in
[
CGAdS17
]
, Gallauer–Choudhury have shown that the motivic Galois group constructed by Ayoub in
[
Ayo14b
,
Ayo14c
]
using the triangulated category of étale motives is isomorphic to the motivic Galois group obtained by Nori’s construction.

Content of this paper

Let us now describe more precisely the content of our paper. Given a
k
-variety
X
, consider the bounded derived category
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
of sheaves of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces with algebraically constructible cohomology on the analytic space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
associated with the base change of
X
along
\sigma
and the category of perverse sheaves
\mathscr{P}(X)
which is the heart of the self-dual perverse
t
-structure on
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
introduced in the book
[
BBD82
]
by Beilinson–Bernstein–Deligne. Let
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
be the triangulated category of constructible étale motivic sheaves (with rational coefficients) which is a full triangulated subcategory of the
\mathbf{Q}
-linear counterpart of the stable homotopy category of schemes
\mathbf{SH}(X)
introduced by Morel and Voevodsky (see
[
MV99
,
Voe98
]
and Jardine’s
[
Jar00
]
).
This category has been extensively studied by Ayoub in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
,
Ayo14a
]
and comes with a realization functor

\mathrm{Bti}_{X}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})

(see Ayoub’s
[
Ayo10
]
) and thus, by composing with the perverse cohomology functor, with a homological functor
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
with values in
\mathscr{P}(X)
.

The category of perverse motives considered in the present paper is defined (see the first author’s
2
) as the universal factorization

\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{M}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\mathscr{P}(X)

of
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
, where
\mathscr{M}(X)
is an abelian category,
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}
is a homological functor and
\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}
is a faithful exact functor. This kind of universal construction goes back to Freyd and is recalled in
1
. As we see in
6
,
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves can also be used to defined the category of perverse motives (see
6.3
and
6.11
).

Given a morphism of
k
-varieties
f:X\rightarrow Y
, the four functors

(0.1)

\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(Y,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{P}}^{!}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}^{\mathscr{P}}}

where developed by Verdier
[
Ver95
]
(see also Kashiwara–Schapira’s book
[
KS94
]
) on the model of the theory developed by Artin, Grothendieck et al. for étale and
\ell
-adic sheaves in
[
AGV+73
]
. The nearby and vanishing cycles functors

\Psi_{g}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{\eta},\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{\sigma},\mathbf{Q})\qquad\Phi_{g}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{\sigma},\mathbf{Q})

associated with a morphism
g:X\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
were constructed by Grothendieck in
[
DK73
]
(here
X_{\eta}
denotes the generic fiber and
X_{\sigma}
the special fiber). By a theorem of Gabber, the functors
\psi_{g}:=\Psi_{g}[-1]
and
\phi_{g}:=\Phi_{g}[-1]
are
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures and thus induce exact functors

(0.2)

\psi_{g}:\mathscr{P}(X_{\eta})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X_{\sigma})\qquad\phi_{g}:\mathscr{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X_{\sigma}).

In this work, we prove that
\mathscr{M}(k)
is canonically equivalent to the abelian category
\mathbf{HM}(k)
of Nori motives (see
2.11
) and that the four operations (
0.1
) (for morphisms of quasi-projective
k
-varieties) and the functors (
0.2
) can be canonically lifted along the functors

\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))\xrightarrow{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{P}(X))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{real}}\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q}),

where
\mathrm{real}
is the realization functor of Section 3.1 of the book
[
BBD82
]
by Beilinson–Bernstein–Deligne, which has been shown to be an equivalence in Beilinson’s
[
Bei87c
]
, to (adjoint) triangulated functors

\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Y))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{M}}^{!}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}}}

and to exact functors

\psi_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}:\mathscr{M}(X_{\eta})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma})\qquad\phi_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}:\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma}).

Relying on Ayoub’s
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
and on the compatibility of the Betti realization with the four operations, our strategy consists in establishing enough of the formalism to show that the categories
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
underlie a stable homotopical
2
-functor in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
]
(see
5.1
), so that the rest of the formalism is obtained from
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
.
The existence of the direct image by a closed immersion or the inverse image by a smooth morphism are obtained immediately via the universal property (see
2
). However, to construct the inverse image by a closed immersion (see
4
), we need to develop analogues, for étale motives, of the functors and gluing exact sequences obtained by Beĭlinson in
[
Bei87a
]
. This is done in
3
and uses derivators, and the logarithmic specialization system of Ayoub, see
[
Ayo07b
,
Ayo14a
]
. The proof of the main theorem is carried out in
5
and the most important step is the proof of the existence of the direct image by the projection of the affine line
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{X}
onto its base
X
(see
5.2
). We conclude this section by the aforementioned applications to intersection cohomology and Leray spectral sequences.

In
6
, we show that perverse motives can also be defined using
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves and that they admit a notion of weights. We deduce the existence
of the weight filtration from the properties of Bondarko’s Chow weight structure and from
the Weil conjectures (cf. Théorème 2 of Deligne’s paper
[
Del80
]
).
Then, using the strict support decomposition of pure objects to reduce to the case of a point, we
show that the category of pure objects of a given weight is semi-simple. As
an application, we get the existence of a weight structure on the derived category of
\mathscr{M}(X)
and an arithmetic proof of Zucker’s theorem
[
Zuc79
, Theorem p.416]
for geometric variations of Hodge structures (see
6.28
and
6.29
).

1.
Categorical preliminaries

Let us recall in this section a few universal constructions related to abelian and triangulated categories. They date back to Freyd’s construction of the abelian hull of an additive category, see his paper
[
Fre66
]
and have been considered in many different forms in various works (see e.g. Verdier’s book
[
Ver96
]
, Krause’s
[
Kra98
]
, Prest’s
[
Pre11
]
and the paper
[
BVP18
]

by Barbieri-Viale and Prest).

Let
\matheusm S
be an additive category. Let
\mathbf{Mod}(\matheusm S)
be the category of right
\matheusm S
-modules, that is, the category of additive functors from
\matheusm S^{\mathrm{op}}
to the category
\mathbf{Ab}
of abelian groups. The category
\mathbf{Mod}(\matheusm S)
is abelian and a sequence of right
\matheusm S
-modules

0\rightarrow F^{\prime}\rightarrow F\rightarrow F^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0

is exact if and only if for every
s\in\matheusm S
the sequence of abelian groups

0\rightarrow F^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow F(s)\rightarrow F^{\prime\prime}(s)\rightarrow 0

is exact.

A right
\matheusm S
-module
F
is said to be of finite presentation if there exist objects
s,t
in
\matheusm S
and an exact sequence

\matheusm S(-,s)\rightarrow\matheusm S(-,t)\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0

in
\mathbf{Mod}(\matheusm S)
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\matheusm S
be an additive category. We denote by
\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)
the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Mod}(\matheusm S)
consisting of right
\matheusm S
-modules of finite presentation.

The category
\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)
is an additive category with cokernels (the cokernel of a morphism of right
\matheusm S
-modules of finite presentation is of finite presentation) and the Yoneda functor

h_{\matheusm S}:\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)

is a fully faithful additive functor. Recall that, given a morphism
t\rightarrow s
in
\matheusm S
, a morphism
r\rightarrow t
is called a pseudo-kernel if the sequence

\matheusm S(-,r)\rightarrow\matheusm S(-,t)\rightarrow\matheusm S(-,s)

is exact in
\mathbf{Mod}(\matheusm S)
. The category
\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)
is abelian if and only if
\matheusm S
has pseudo-kernels (see
[
Fre66
, Theorem 1.4]
and

[
Kra98
, Lemma 2.2]
).
It also satisfies the following universal property.

Proposition 1.2
.

(
[
Kra98
, 2.1 and Lemma 2.6]
).
Let
\matheusm S
be an additive category. Let
\matheusm A
be an additive category with cokernels and
F:\matheusm S\rightarrow\matheusm A
be an additive functor, then there exists, up to a natural isomorphism, a unique right exact functor
\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)\rightarrow\matheusm A
that extends
F
. Moreover, if
\matheusm S
and
\matheusm A
admit pseudo-kernels,
then this functor is exact if and only if
F
preserves pseudo-kernels.

Note that the construction can be dualized so that there is a universal way to add kernels to an additive category. One simply set
\mathbf{L}(\matheusm S):=\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
.
The two constructions can be combined to add both cokernels and kernels at the same time. Let
\matheusm S
be an additive category and let

\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S):=\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)).

Then the functor
h:\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S)
is a fully faithful additive functor and
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S)
is an abelian category which enjoys the following universal property (this is Freyd’s abelian hull).

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
\matheusm A
be an abelian category and
F:\matheusm S\rightarrow\matheusm A
be an additive functor, then there exists, up to a natural isomorphism, a unique exact functor
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S)\rightarrow\matheusm A
that extends
F
.

Note also that the category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S)
is canonically equivalent to
\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{L}(\matheusm S))
.

This construction can be used to provide an alternative description of Nori’s category (see the paper
[
BVP18
]
by Barbieri-Viale and Prest). Let
\matheusm Q
be a quiver,
\matheusm A
be an abelian category and
T:\matheusm Q\rightarrow\matheusm A
be a representation. Let
\mathbf{P}(\matheusm Q)
be the path category and
\mathbf{P}(\matheusm Q)^{\oplus}
be its additive completion obtained by adding finite direct sums.
Then, up to natural isomorphisms, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\matheusm Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{P}(\matheusm Q)^{\oplus}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathbf{P}(\matheusm Q)^{\oplus})=:\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{T}}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A}}

where
\varrho_{T}
is an additive functor and
\rho_{T}
an exact functor. The kernel of
\rho_{T}
is a thick subcategory of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)
and we define the abelian category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)
to be the quotient of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)
by this kernel. By construction, the functor
\rho_{T}
has a canonical factorization

\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)\xrightarrow{\pi_{T}}\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,H)\xrightarrow{r_{T}}\matheusm A

where
\pi_{T}
is an exact functor and
r_{T}
is a faithful exact functor. If we denote by
\overline{T}
the composition of the representation
\matheusm Q\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)
and the functor
\pi_{T}:\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q)\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)
, it provides a canonical factorization of
T
:

\matheusm Q\xrightarrow{\overline{T}}\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)\xrightarrow{r_{T}}\matheusm A

where
\overline{T}
is a representation and
r_{T}
is a faithful exact functor. It is easy to see that the above factorization is universal among all factorizations of
T
of the form

\matheusm Q\xrightarrow{R}\matheusm B\xrightarrow{s}\matheusm A

where
\matheusm B
is an abelian category,
R
is a representation and
s
is a faithful exact functor. In particular, whenever Nori’s construction is available, e.g. if
\matheusm A
is Noetherian, Artinian and has finite dimensional
\mathrm{Hom}
-groups over
\mathbf{Q}
(see the paper
[
Ivo17
]
by the first author), then the category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)
is equivalent to Nori’s abelian category associated with the quiver representation
T
.

Let us consider the case when
\matheusm Q
is an additive category and
T
is an additive functor. Then, up to natural isomorphisms, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\matheusm Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{*}}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A}}

where
T^{*}
is an exact functor. The kernel of
T^{*}
is a thick subcategory of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q)
and we define the abelian category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q,T)
to be the quotient of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q)
by this kernel.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
\matheusm Q
and
\matheusm A
be additive categories. Then, for every additive functor
T:\matheusm Q\rightarrow\matheusm A
, the categories
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q,T)
are canonically equivalent.

Proof.

To see this, it suffices to check that the factorization

\matheusm Q\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q,T)\rightarrow\matheusm A

satisfies the universal property that defines
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm Q,T)
. Consider a factorization of the representation
T
of the quiver
\matheusm Q

\matheusm Q\xrightarrow{R}\matheusm B\xrightarrow{s}\matheusm A

where
\matheusm B
is an abelian category,
R
is a representation and
s
is a faithful exact functor. Since
s
is faithful,
R
must be an additive functor. Therefore, we get a commutative diagram (up to natural isomorphisms)

\textstyle{{\matheusm Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
exact
\scriptstyle{T^{*}}
\textstyle{{\matheusm B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A.}}

The exactness and the faithfulness of
s
imply that the above diagram can be further completed into a
commutative diagram (up to natural isomorphisms)

\textstyle{{\matheusm Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
exact
\scriptstyle{T^{*}}
\textstyle{{\matheusm B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm Q,T)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A.}}

This shows the desired universal property.
∎

Let us finally consider the special case when
\matheusm S
is a triangulated category. In that case the additive category
\matheusm S
has pseudo-kernels and pseudo-cokernels, in particular,
the category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S):=\mathbf{R}(\matheusm S)

is an abelian category.
1
1

1

This is the abelian category denoted by
A(\matheusm S)
in Chapter V of Neeman’s book
[
Nee01
]
.

The Yoneda embedding
h_{\matheusm S}:\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)
is a homological functor and is universal for this property (see
[
Nee01
, Theorem 5.1.18]
). In particular, if
\matheusm A
is an abelian category, any homological functor
H:\matheusm S\rightarrow\matheusm A
admits a canonical factorization

\textstyle{{\matheusm S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{\matheusm S}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{H}}
\textstyle{\matheusm A}

where
\rho_{H}
is an exact functor. This factorization of
H
is universal among all such factorizations.

The kernel of
\rho_{H}
is a thick subcategory of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)
and we define the abelian category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)
to be the quotient of
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)
by this kernel. By construction, the functor
\rho_{H}
has a canonical factorization

\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)\xrightarrow{\pi_{H}}\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)\xrightarrow{r_{H}}\matheusm A

where
\pi_{H}
is an exact functor and
r_{H}
is a faithful exact functor. Setting
H_{\matheusm S}:=\pi_{H}\circ h_{\matheusm S}
, it provides a canonical factorization of
H
:

\matheusm S\xrightarrow{H_{\matheusm S}}\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)\xrightarrow{r_{H}}\matheusm A

where
H_{\matheusm S}
is a homological functor and
r_{H}
a faithful exact functor. It is easy to see that the above factorization is universal among all factorizations of
H
of the form

\matheusm S\xrightarrow{L}\matheusm B\xrightarrow{s}\matheusm A

where
L
is a homological functor and
s
is a faithful exact functor.

We can also see the triangulated category
\matheusm S
simply as a quiver (resp. an additive category) and the homological functor
H:\matheusm S\rightarrow\matheusm A
simply as a representation (resp. an additive functor). In particular, we have at our disposal the universal factorizations of the representation
H
:

\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm S,H)\rightarrow\matheusm A

and

\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S,H)\rightarrow\matheusm A

where the arrows on the right are exact and faithful functors.

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
\matheusm S
be a triangulated category,
\matheusm A
be an abelian category and
H:\matheusm S\rightarrow\matheusm A
be a homological functor. Then, the three abelian categories
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm S,H)
,
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S,H)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)
are canonically equivalent.

Proof.

We have seen in
1.4
that
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\matheusm S,H)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S,H)
are canonically equivalent. Let us prove that so do
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S,H)
and
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)
. It suffices to check that the factorization

\matheusm S\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)\rightarrow\matheusm A

satisfies the universal property that defines
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\matheusm S,H)
. Consider a factorization of the additive functor
H
:

\matheusm Q\xrightarrow{R}\matheusm B\xrightarrow{s}\matheusm A

where
\matheusm B
is an abelian category,
R
is an additive functor and
s
is a faithful exact functor. Since
s
is faithful,
R
must be homological. Therefore, we get a commutative diagram (up to natural isomorphisms)

\textstyle{{\matheusm S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
exact
\textstyle{{\matheusm B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A.}}

The exactness and the faithfulness of
s
imply that the above diagram can be further completed into a
commutative diagram (up to natural isomorphisms)

\textstyle{{\matheusm S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
exact
\textstyle{{\matheusm B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,T)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\matheusm A.}}

This shows the desired universal property.
∎

2.
Perverse motives

We fix a field
k
that admits an embedding
\sigma:k\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
.
Unless otherwise specified, we will only consider quasi-projective

k
-varieties in this article.

2.1.
Definition

Let
X
be a quasi-projective
k
-variety. We denote by
X^{\mathrm{an}}
the complex
analytic space associated with the base change of
X
along

\sigma
, by
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
the category of complexes of sheaves of

\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
with bounded algebraically
constructible cohomology,
by
\mathscr{P}(X)
the heart of the perverse t-structure on
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})

introduced in Section 2 of the book
[
BBD82
]
of Beilinson–Bernstein–Deligne for the self-dual perversity and
by
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
the triangulated category of constructible étale
motivic sheaves with rational coefficients (see for example
Section 3 of Ayoub’s paper
[
Ayo14a
]
).
By
[
CD19
, Theorem 16.2.18]
, this last category is equivalent
to the category of constructible Beĭlinson motives studied in Cisinski and
Déglise’s book
[
CD19
]
, and the equivalence commutes with the operations
we will consider later (direct and inverse images and tensor product).
So we will use reference to Ayoub’s articles or to the book
[
CD19
]
,
as convenient.

To construct the abelian category of perverse motives
\mathscr{M}(X)
used in the present work, we take
\matheusm S
to be the triangulated category
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
and
H
to be the homological functor
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
obtained by composing of the Betti realization

\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}:\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})

constructed by Ayoub in
[
Ayo10
]
and the perverse cohomology functor
{}^{p}H^{0}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X)
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety. The abelian category of perverse motives is the abelian category

\mathscr{M}(X):=\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\matheusm S,H)=\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X),{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}).

By construction the functor
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
has a factorization

\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{M}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\mathscr{P}(X)

where
\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}
is a faithful exact functor and
{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}
is a homological functor. Let us recall the two consequences (denoted by
P1
and
P2
below) of the universal property of the factorization

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(X).}

The property
P1
below is proved in
[
Ivo17
, Proposition 6.6]
. A proof of property
P2
can be found in Proposition 2.5 of the paper
[
IY18
]
by
Ivorra–Yamazaki.

P1
For every commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(Y)}

where
\mathsf{F}
is a triangulated functor,
\mathsf{G}
is an exact functor and
\alpha
is an invertible natural transformation, there exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\beta}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{E}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(Y)}

is an exact functor and

are invertible natural transformations such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\beta}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{E}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{G}}

\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}}

is commutative.

P2

Let

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

2.2.
Lifting of
2
-functors

As in
[
Ayo07a
, §1.1]
, in this work, we only consider strict
2
-categories. However, as in loc.cit.,
2
-functors are not necessarily strict (see also Deligne’s
[
Del
]
).

Let
(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
be the category of quasi-projective
k
-varieties and
\matheusm C
be a subcategory of
(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
.
The properties
P1
and
P2
can be used to lift (covariant or contravariant)
2
-functors. Indeed, let
\mathsf{F}:\matheusm C\rightarrow\mathfrak{TR}
be a
2
-functor (let’s say covariant to fix the notation), where
\mathfrak{TR}
is the

2
-category of triangulated categories,
such that
\mathsf{F}(X)=\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
for every
k
-variety
X
in
\matheusm C
. Similarly, let
\mathfrak{Ab}
be the
2
-category of abelian categories, and
let
\mathsf{G}:\matheusm C\rightarrow\mathfrak{Ab}
be a
2
-functor such that
\mathsf{G}(X)=\mathscr{P}(X)
for every
k
-variety
X
in
\matheusm C

and that
G(f)
is exact for every morphism
f
in
\matheusm C
.

We have forgetful functors from
\mathfrak{TR}
and
\mathfrak{Ab}
to the
2
-category of additive categories.
Assume that
(\Theta,\alpha):\mathsf{F}\rightarrow\mathsf{G}
is a
1
-morphism of
2
-functors, where we see
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{G}
as
2
-functors into the

2
-category of additive categories via these forgetful functors,
such that
\Theta_{X}={}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
for every
X\in\matheusm C
and that
\alpha_{f}
is invertible for every morphism
f
in
\matheusm C
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\matheusm C
. By applying
P1
to the square

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}(f)}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{G}(f)}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(Y)}

we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\beta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{E}(f)}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{G}(f)}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Using the uniqueness and the fact that the functors
\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}
, for
X
in
\matheusm C
, are faithful it is easy to see that the morphisms
c_{\mathsf{E}}
satisfy the cocycle condition. Hence
\mathsf{E}:\matheusm C\rightarrow\mathfrak{Ab}
is a
2
-functor and
({}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}},\beta)
,
(\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}},\gamma)
are
1
-morphisms of
2
-functors, where again we see the
2
-functors as functors into the
2
-category of
additive categories. As
1
- and
2
-morphisms in
\mathfrak{Ab}
are the same as
1
- and
2
-morphisms in the
2
-category of additive categories,
the morphism
(\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}},\gamma)
is also a
1
-morphism of
2
-functors
\matheusm C\rightarrow\mathfrak{Ab}
.

2.3.
Betti realization of étale motives

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of quasi-projective
k
-varieties.
Recall that the category
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the derived category of the abelian category of perverse sheaves on
X
via the realization functor constructed in
[
BBD82
, 3.1.9]
(it is known to be an equivalence by
[
Bei87c
, Theorem 1.3]
). In particular, the four (adjoint) functors

\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(Y,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{P}}^{!}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}^{\mathscr{P}}}

can be seen as functors between the derived categories of perverse sheaves (for their construction in terms of perverse sheaves see
[
Bei87c
]
).

Let
\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}:\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
be the realization functor of
[
Ayo10
]
. If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a morphism of quasi-projective
k
-varieties, by construction, there is an invertible natural transformation

\theta_{f}:f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}\circ f^{*}

(see
[
Ayo10
, Proposition 2.4]
). Let
\theta
be the collection of these natural transformations, then
(\mathrm{Bti}^{*},\theta)
is a morphism of stable homotopical
2
-functors in the sense of
[
Ayo10
, Definition 3.1]
. Following the notation in
[
Ayo10
]
, we denote by

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}

\displaystyle:\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\circ f_{*}\rightarrow f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}\;;

\displaystyle\rho_{f}

\displaystyle:f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\circ f_{!}\;;

\displaystyle\xi_{f}

\displaystyle:\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}\circ f^{!}\rightarrow f^{!}_{\mathscr{P}}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\;;

the induced natural transformations. By
[
Ayo10
, Théorème 3.19]
these transformations are invertible.

2.4.
Direct images under affine and quasi-finite morphisms

Let
{}^{\mathrm{QAff}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
be the subcategory of
(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
with the same objects but in which we only retain the morphisms that are quasi-finite and affine. By
[
BBD82
, Corollaire 4.1.3]
, for such a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, the functors

f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*},f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(Y,\mathbf{Q})

are
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures. In particular, they induce exact functors between categories of perverse sheaves and by applying the property
P1
to the canonical transformation
\gamma_{f}:\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\circ f_{*}\rightarrow f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}
,
we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(Y)}

where
f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}
is an exact functor and
\gamma^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f},\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}
are invertible natural transformations such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

is commutative. Moreover, since the natural transformations

are compatible with the composition of morphisms (that is, with the connection
2
-isomorphisms),

2.2
provides a
2
-functor

{}^{\mathrm{QAff}}\mathsf{H}_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}:{}^{\mathrm{QAff}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)\rightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

-functors.
For every affine and quasi-finite morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
we have a natural transformation

\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}:\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}\rightarrow f_{*}^{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}

compatible with the composition of morphisms.

2.5.
Inverse image by a smooth morphism

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth morphism of
k
-varieties. Then,
\Omega_{f}
is a locally free
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module of finite rank. Let
d_{f}
its rank (which is constant on each connected component of
X
). Then,
d_{f}
is the relative dimension of
f
(see Grothendieck’s EGA IV, more precisely
[
Gro67
, (17.10.2)]
) and if
g:Y\rightarrow Z
is a smooth morphism, then
d_{g\circ f}=d_{g}+d_{f}
with the obvious abuse of notation (see
[
Gro67
, (17.10.3)]
). By
[
BBD82
, 4.2.4]
, the functor

f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d_{f}]:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(Y,\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})

is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures. In particular, it induces an exact functor between the categories of perverse sheaves and by applying the property
P1
to the canonical transformation
\theta_{f}:f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}\circ f^{*}
,
we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern 8.0pt\theta^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern 8.0pt\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}(X)}

where the functor in the middle
f_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}[d_{f}]
is an exact functor and
\theta^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f},\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}
are invertible natural transformations such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d_{f}]}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta^{\mathbf{DA}}_{f}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\rho_{Y}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}[d_{f}]}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\rho_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}}

is commutative.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that
f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A
, given
A
in
\mathscr{M}(Y)
, is not yet defined.
We define the function
f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
by setting
f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}:=(f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}[d_{f}])[-d_{f}]
.

Let
{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
be the subcategory of
(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
with the same objects but having as morphisms only the smooth morphisms of
k
-varieties.
Since the natural transformations
\theta_{f}
are compatible with the composition of morphisms (that is, with the connection
2
-isomorphisms),
2.2
provides a contravariant
2
-functor

{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}:{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)\rightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

with
{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(X)=\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
and such that
({}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}},\theta^{\mathbf{DA}})
and
(\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}},\theta^{\mathscr{M}})
are
1
-morphisms of
2
-functors. For every smooth morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
we have a natural transformation

\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}:f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}

compatible with the composition of morphisms.

2.6.
Exchange structure

Let us denote by

{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}:{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)\rightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

the restriction of the
2
-functor obtained in
2.4
to the subcategory
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
of
(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
with the same objects but having as morphisms only the closed immersions of
k
-varieties.
Exchange structures are defined in Définition 1.2.1 of
[
Ayo07a
]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

The exchange structure
Ex^{*}_{*}
on
({}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{P}},{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}_{*}^{\mathscr{P}})
with respect to cartesian squares can be lifted to an exchange structure on the pair
({}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}},{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}_{*}^{\mathscr{M}})
.

Proof.

The proposition is a simple application of property
P2
. Consider a cartesian square

\textstyle{{X^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{Y}

such that
i
is a closed immersion and
f
is a smooth morphism (more generally
i
need not be a closed immersion and can be any quasi-finite affine morphism). Note that, since the morphism
i^{\prime*}\Omega^{1}_{f}\rightarrow\Omega^{1}_{f^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism,
f
and
f^{\prime}
have the same relative dimension
d
.
Let
Ex_{*}^{*}:f^{*}i_{*}\rightarrow i^{\prime}_{*}f^{\prime*}
and
{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex_{*}^{*}:f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\rightarrow i^{\prime\mathscr{P}}_{*}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{P}}
the exchange
2
-isomorphisms in
\mathbf{DA}(-)
and
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{P}(-))
.
We have to construct a
2
-isomorphism

\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern 24.0pt{}^{\mathscr{M}}Ex^{*}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Y^{\prime}))}}

which is compatible with
{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex_{*}^{*}
via the
2
-isomorphisms
\gamma_{g}^{\mathscr{M}},\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{g^{\prime}}
and
\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f},\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f^{\prime}}
.
This amounts to constructing a
2
-isomorphism

\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}[d]i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{M}}[d]}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern 24.0pt{}^{\mathscr{M}}Ex^{*}_{*}[d]}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y^{\prime})}}

such that

\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle(\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{i})^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}[d]}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d]}
\textstyle{{}_{\kern-48.0pt{}^{\mathscr{M}}Ex^{*}_{*}[d]}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{}_{\kern 24.0pt{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex^{*}_{*}[d]}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{M}}[d]}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d]}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle(\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{i^{\prime}})^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y^{\prime})}}

is commutative. Note that such a
2
-isomorphism is necessarily unique since the functors
\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}
, for
S
a
k
-variety, are faithful. For the same reason they will also be compatible with the horizontal and vertical compositions of mixed squares (see
[
Ayo07a
, Définition 1.21]
). The proposition follows from property
P2
applied to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle(\gamma_{i})^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}[d]}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d]}
\textstyle{{}_{\kern-48.0ptEx^{*}_{*}[d]}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{{}_{\kern 24.0pt{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex^{*}_{*}[d]}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}[d]}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta_{f^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{P}}[d]}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle(\gamma_{i^{\prime}})^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(X^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime\mathscr{P}}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{P}(Y^{\prime}).}}

The commutativity of this diagram follows from the compatibility of the Betti realization with the exchange structures.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

The application of property
P2
ensures that the two exchange structures
{}^{\mathscr{M}}Ex^{*}_{*}
and
{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex^{*}_{*}
are compatible with the canonical
2
-isomorphisms
\theta^{\mathscr{M}}
. That is, the diagram

\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\mathscr{P}}Ex^{*}_{*}}
\textstyle{{i^{\prime\mathscr{P}}_{*}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y^{\prime}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\mathscr{M}}Ex^{*}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y^{\prime}}i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma^{\mathscr{M}}_{i^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{i^{\prime\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X^{\prime}}f^{\prime*}_{\mathscr{M}}}

is commutative. This follows from the faithfulness of the functors
\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}
after applying the shift functor
(-)[d]
.

2.7.
Adjunction

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be an affine and étale morphism. In that case the exact functors
f_{*}:\mathscr{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(Y)
and
f_{!}:\mathscr{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(Y)
are respectively right and left adjoint to the exact functor
f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}:\mathscr{P}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X)
. We can use the property
P2
to lift these adjunctions to the functors
f^{\mathscr{M}}_{!},f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}},f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be an affine and étale morphism.

(1)

There exist unique natural transformations
\mathrm{Id}\rightarrow f_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
and
f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}
such that the squares

\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}f_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{f_{*}^{\mathscr{P}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}}

\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}f^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}f^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

are commutative. With these natural transformations, the functors
(f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}},f_{*}^{\mathscr{M}})
form a pair of adjoint functors.

(2)

There exist unique natural transformations
\mathrm{Id}\rightarrow f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}}
and
f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}
such that the squares

\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}f^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}f_{!}^{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}
\textstyle{{f_{!}^{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{f_{!}^{\mathscr{P}}f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

are commutative. With these natural transformations, the functors
(f_{!}^{\mathscr{M}},f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}})
form a pair of adjoint functors.

Proof.

As for
2.3
, the proof is a simple application of property
P2
. The details are left to the reader.
∎

2.8.
Duality

The result in this subsection will be used in the proof of
5.3
. Let
\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}
be the duality functor for perverse sheaves and
\varepsilon^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}:\mathrm{Id}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}
be the canonical
2
-isomorphism. Recall that, given a smooth morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of relative dimension
d
, there is a canonical
2
-isomorphism

\varepsilon^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}:\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}(-)(d)[d]\rightarrow f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}(-)[d]\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{Y}.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
X,Y
be
k
-varieties and
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth morphism of relative dimension
d
.

(1)

There exist a contravariant exact functor
\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}:\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X)
, a
2
-isomorphism
\nu^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}:\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\rightarrow\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}
and a
2
-isomorphism
\varepsilon^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}:\mathrm{Id}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{X}^{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{X}^{\mathscr{M}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}

is commutative.

(2)

There exists a
2
-isomorphism

\varepsilon^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}:\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)(d)[d]\rightarrow f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)[d]\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}

such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}(-)(d)[d]\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}(-)[d]\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{Y}\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{P}}_{X}\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)(d)[d]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{f^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}(-)[d]\circ\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)(d)[d]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rat}^{\mathscr{M}}_{X}\circ f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)[d]\circ\mathbb{D}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Y}}

is commutative.

Proof.

Again, the proof is a simple application of property
P2
, once we
know the existence and properties of the Verdier duality functor
on motives. We give references for these properties and
leave the rest of the details to the reader.

By
[
Ayo07a
, Théorème 2.3.75]
and
[
Ayo07b
, §4.5]
(see also
[
CD19
, Théorème 3 and Théorème 7 in the introduction]
),
the categories
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
are symmetric monoïdal closed and we have
Verdier duality functors
\mathbb{D}_{X}
such that, for

f:X\rightarrow Y
a morphism of quasi-projective
k
-varieties, there is a
canonical isomorphism
f^{*}\circ\mathbb{D}_{Y}\simeq\mathbb{D}_{X}\circ f^{!}
. If
f
is smooth of relative dimension

d
, the functor
f^{!}
is defined in
[
Ayo07a
, §1.5.3.1]
as the composition
\mathsf{Th}(\Omega_{f})\circ f^{*}
, where
\mathsf{Th}(\Omega_{f})
is the Thom equivalence associated with the locally free
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module
\Omega_{f}
. As
\Omega_{f}
has rank
d
, we get an isomorphism
f^{!}\simeq f^{*}(d)[2d]
by
[
Ayo14a
, corollaire 2.14]
(see also property 4 of
[
CD19
, A.5.1]
). Hence, we get an isomorphism

f^{*}[d]\circ\mathbb{D}_{Y}\simeq\mathbb{D}_{X}\circ f^{*}(d)[d]
.
Moreover, the Betti realization functors

\mathrm{Bti}_{X}
are symmetric monoïdal unital (see

[
Ayo10
, Remarque 3.3]
), and they commute with
internal
\mathrm{Hom}
s on constructible objects by

[
Ayo10
, Théorème 3.19]
; so it commutes with the
Verdier duality functor on constructible objects,
as that functor is constructed using the
four operations and the internal
\mathrm{Hom}
(see for example

[
CD19
, A.5.2]
). The last crucial observation is that Verdier duality
on
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
restricts to an exact contravariant functor on the
subcategory of perverse sheaves (see for example the beginning
of Section 4 of
[
BBD82
]
).
∎

2.9.
Perverse motives as a stack

Let
S
be a quasi-projective
k
-variety. Let us denote by
\mathsf{AffEt}/S
the category of affine étale schemes over
S
endowed with the étale topology. As in
[
Sta18
, Tag 02XU]
, the
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{AffEt}/X

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{Ab}

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathscr{M}(U)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto u^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}

can be turned into a fibered category
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{AffEt}/S
such that the fiber over an object
U
of
\mathsf{AffEt}/X
is the category
\mathscr{M}(U)
.

Proposition 2.7
.

The fibered category
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{AffEt}/S
is a stack for the étale topology.

Proof.

Let
U
be a
k
-variety,
I
be a finite set and
\mathscr{U}=(u_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow U)_{i\in I}
be a covering of
U
by affine and étale morphisms. If
J\subseteq I
is a nonempty subset of
I
, we denote by
U_{J}
the fiber product of the
U_{j}
,
j\in J
, over
U
and by
u_{J}:U_{J}\rightarrow U
the induced morphism. Given an object
A\in\mathscr{M}(U)
, and
k\in\mathbf{Z}
, we set

C^{k}(A,\mathscr{U}):=\begin{cases}0&${if $k<0$;}$\\
\bigoplus_{J\subseteq I,|J|=k+1}(u_{J})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(u_{J})_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A&\textrm{if $k\geqslant 0$.}\end{cases}

We make
C^{\bullet}(A,\mathscr{U})
into a complex using the alternating sum of the maps obtained from the unit of the adjunction in
2.5
. The unit of this adjunction also provides a canonical morphism
A\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(A,\mathscr{U})
in
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(U))
. This morphism induces a quasi-isomorphism on the underlying complex of perverse sheaves and so is a quasi-isomorphism itself since the forgetful functor to the derived category of perverse sheaves is conservative.

By
[
Sta18
, Tag 0268]
, to prove the proposition we have to show the following:

(1)

if
U
is an object in
\mathsf{AffEt}/S
and
A,B
are objects in
\mathscr{M}(U)
, then the presheaf
(V\xrightarrow{v}U)\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}(V)}(v_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A,v^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}B)
on
\mathsf{AffEt}/U
is a sheaf for the étale topology;

(2)

for any covering
\mathscr{U}=(u_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow U)_{i\in I}
of the site
\mathsf{AffEt}/S
, any descent datum is effective.

We already now that the similar assertions are true for perverse sheaves by
[
BBD82
, Proposition 3.2.2, Théorème 3.2.4]
. Let
\mathscr{U}=(u_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow U)_{i\in I}
be a covering in the site
\mathsf{AffEt}/S
. Given
i,j\in I
, we denote by
u_{ij}:U_{ij}:=U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}\rightarrow U
the fiber product and by
p_{ij}:U_{ij}\rightarrow U_{i}
,
p_{ji}:U_{ij}\rightarrow U_{j}
the projections.

Let us first prove (1). Let
A,B
be objects in
\mathscr{M}(U)
and
K,L
be their underlying perverse sheaves. Consider the canonical commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}((u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,(u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\prod_{i,j\in I}\mathrm{Hom}((u_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,(u_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Hom}(K,L)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}((u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}K,(u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}L)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{i,j\in I}\mathrm{Hom}((u_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}K,(u_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}L).}

The lower row is exact and the vertical arrows are injective. We only have to check that the upper row is exact at the middle term. Let
c
be an element in
\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}((u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,(u_{i})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}B)
which belongs to the equalizer of the two maps on the right-hand side. Then, it defines (by adjunction) a morphism
c_{0}
and a morphism
c_{1}
such that the square

(2.1)

\textstyle{{C^{0}(A,\mathscr{U})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{c_{0}}
\textstyle{{C^{1}(A,\mathscr{U})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\textstyle{{C^{0}(B,\mathscr{U})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{C^{1}(B,\mathscr{U})}

is commutative. Since
A\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(A,\mathscr{U})
and
B\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(B,\mathscr{U})
are quasi-isomorphisms,
A
is the kernel of the upper map in (
2.1
) and
B
is the kernel of the lower map. Hence,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
induce a morphism
A\rightarrow B
in
\mathscr{M}(U)
which maps to
c
.

Now we prove (2). Consider a descent datum. In other words, consider, for every
i\in I
, an object
A_{i}
in
\mathscr{M}(U_{i})
and, for every
i,j\in I
, an isomorphism
\phi_{ij}:(p_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A_{i}\rightarrow(p_{ji})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A_{j}
in
\mathscr{M}(U_{ij})
satisfying the usual cocycle condition. Let
A
the kernel of the map

\bigoplus_{i\in I}(u_{i})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}A_{i}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}(u_{i})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(p_{ij})_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}(p_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A_{i}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}(u_{ij})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(p_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A_{i}

given on
(u_{i})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}A_{i}
by the difference of the maps obtained by composing the morphism induced by adjunction

(u_{i})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}A_{i}\rightarrow(u_{i})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(p_{ij})_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}(p_{ij})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A_{i}

with either the identity or the isomorphism
\phi_{ij}
. Using the fact that descent data on perverse sheaves are effective, it is easy to see that
A
makes the given descent datum effective.
∎

2.10.
A simpler generating quiver

Let
X
be a
k
-variety. Consider the quiver
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
defined as follows. A vertex in
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
is a triple
(a:Y\rightarrow X,Z,n)
where
a:Y\rightarrow X
is morphism of
k
-varieties,
Z
is a closed subscheme of
Y
and
n\in\mathbf{Z}
is an integer.

•

Let
(Y_{1},Z_{1},i)
and
(Y_{2},Z_{2},i)
be vertices in
\mathbf{Pairs}_{X}^{\mathrm{eff}}
. Then, every morphism of
X
-schemes
f:Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
such that
f(Z_{1})\subseteq Z_{2}
defines an edge

(2.2)

f:(Y_{1},Z_{1},i)\rightarrow(Y_{2},Z_{2},i).

•

For every vertex
(a:Y\rightarrow X,Z,i)
in
\mathbf{Pairs}_{X}^{\mathrm{eff}}
and every closed subscheme
W\subseteq Z
, we have an edge

(2.3)

\partial:(a:Y\rightarrow X,Z,i)\rightarrow(az:Z\rightarrow X,W,i-1)

where
z:Z\hookrightarrow Y
is the closed immersion.

The quiver
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
admits a natural representation in
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
. If
c=(a:Y\rightarrow X,Z,i)
is a vertex in the quiver
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
and
u:U\hookrightarrow Y
is the inclusion of the complement of
Z
in
Y
, then we set

B(c):=a^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{U}[-i]

where
K_{U}
is the dualizing complex of
U
.

Remark 2.8
.

There is a difference between the representation
{}^{p}H^{0}\circ\overline{B}
used here and the representation used in
[
Ivo17
, 7.2–7.4]
(see
[
Ivo17
, Remark 7.8]
). In loc.cit. the relative dualizing complex
u^{!}_{\mathscr{P}}a^{!}_{\mathscr{P}}\mathbf{Q}_{X}
is used instead of the absolute dualizing complex
K_{U}
. If
X
is smooth, then the two different choices lead to equivalent categories.

On vertices the representation
B
is defined as follows. Let
c_{1}:=(a_{1}:Y_{1}\rightarrow X,Z_{1},i)
,
c_{2}:=(a_{2}:Y_{2}\rightarrow X,Z_{2},i)
be vertices in
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
and
f:c_{1}\rightarrow c_{2}
be an edge of type (
2.2
). The morphism
f
maps
Z_{1}
to
Z_{2}
and therefore
U:=f^{-1}(U_{2})
is contained in
U_{1}
. Let
u:U\hookrightarrow U_{1}
be the open immersion. Then, we have a morphism

f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{1*}K_{U_{1}}\xrightarrow{\textrm{adj.}}f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}(u_{1}u)^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{U}\longrightarrow u^{\mathscr{P}}_{2*}f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}K_{U}=u^{\mathscr{P}}_{2*}f^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}f^{!}_{\mathscr{P}}K_{U_{2}}\xrightarrow{\textrm{adj.}}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{2*}K_{U_{2}}

where the arrow in the middle is given by the exchange morphism. By taking the image of this morphism under
a_{2!}[-i]
, we get a morphism

B(f):B(c_{1}):=a^{\mathscr{P}}_{1!}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{1*}K_{U_{1}}[-i]\rightarrow B(c_{2}):=a^{\mathscr{P}}_{2!}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{2*}K_{U_{2}}[-i].

Let
c=(Y\xrightarrow{a}X,Z,i)
be a vertex in
\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
, and
W\subseteq Z
be a closed subset.
Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{U:=Y\setminus Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{Y\setminus W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{Y}}
\textstyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{{V:=Z\setminus W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{z_{V}}
\textstyle{{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}
\scriptstyle{b}

where
v,v_{Y},j
are the open immersions,
z
the closed immersion and
a,b
the structural morphisms. The localization triangle

(z_{V})^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}(z_{V})_{\mathscr{P}}^{!}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}j^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}\xrightarrow{+1},

applied to
K_{Y\setminus W}
, provides a morphism

j^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{U}\rightarrow(z_{V})^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}K_{V}[1].

As
z
and
z_{V}
are closed immersions, applying
(v_{Y})_{*}
, yields a morphism

u^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{U}\rightarrow z^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}v^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{V}[1].

Applying
a_{!}[-i]
, we obtain a morphism

B(\partial):B(c):=a^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}u^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{U}[-i]\rightarrow B(az:Z\rightarrow X,Z,i-1):=b^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}v^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}K_{V}[1-i].

The category of perverse Nori motives considered in
[
Ivo17
]
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
X
be a
k
