-variety. The category of effective perverse Nori motives is the abelian category

\mathscr{N}^{\mathrm{eff}}(X):=\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X},{}^{p}H^{0}\circ B).

Recall that the category
\mathscr{M}(X)
can also be obtained by considering
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
simply as a quiver, that is it is canonically equivalent to the abelian category
\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{qv}}(\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X),{}^{p}H^{0}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X})
(see
1.5
). The Grothendieck six operations formalism constructed in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
and its compatibility with its topological counterpart on the triangulated categories
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
shown in
[
Ayo10
]
, imply that the quiver representation
B

can be lifted via the realization functor
\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}
to a quiver representation

\overline{B}:\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X).

In particular, since the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X},}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{B}}
\scriptstyle{B}

is commutative (up to natural isomorphisms),
there exists a canonical faithful exact functor

(2.4)

\mathscr{N}^{\mathrm{eff}}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X).

Let us explain now how Tate twists can be defined in the categories
\mathscr{N}^{\mathrm{eff}}(X)
and
\mathscr{M}(X)
. In the category
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
, the Tate twist
(-)(1)
is defined to be the endofunctor
\mathsf{Th}(\mathscr{O}_{X})(-)[-2]
where
\mathsf{Th}(\mathscr{O}_{X})
is the Thom equivalence associated with the trivial locally free sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{X}
(see
[
Ayo07a
, §1.5.3]
). This construction, being compatible with the usual Tate twist via the Betti realization, induces an exact functor
(-)(1)
on the category
\mathscr{M}(X)
. Note that this functor is an equivalence by construction.

In the category
\mathscr{N}^{\mathrm{eff}}(X)
Tate twists can be defined using the following observation: if
S
is a
k
-variety,
q:\mathbf{G}_{m,S}\rightarrow S
is the structural morphism and
v:V\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{m,S}
is the complement of the unit section, then
q_{!}v_{*}v^{*}q^{!}K=K(1)[1]
for every
K\in\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(S,\mathbf{Q})
. In particular, if
Q:\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{Pairs}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{X}
is the morphism of quivers which maps

(Y,Z,n)
to
(\mathbf{G}_{m,Y},\mathbf{G}_{m,Z}\cup Y,n+1)
(here
Y
is embedded in
\mathbf{G}_{m,Y}
via the unit section), then one has a natural isomorphism between
B(Q(Y,Z,n))
and
B(Y,Z,n)(1)
. As a consequence, the Tate twist on the category of effective perverse Nori motives can be defined as the exact functor induced by the morphism of quivers
Q
(and the usual Tate twist).

This last construction does not yield an equivalence and one defines the category
\mathscr{N}(X)
to be the category obtained from
\mathscr{N}^{\mathrm{eff}}(X)
by inverting the Tate twists (see
[
Ivo17
, 7.6]
for details). By construction, the category of Nori motives
\mathbf{HM}(k)
of Nori’s work
[
Fak00
]
coincides with
\mathscr{N}(k)
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The functor (
2.4
) extends to a faithful exact

(2.5)

\mathscr{N}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X).

Proof.

To prove the lemma it is enough to observe that
there is a natural isomorphism in
\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)
between
\overline{B}(Q(Y,Z,n))
and
\overline{B}(Y,Z,n)(1)
.
∎

Proposition 2.11
.

The category
\mathscr{M}(k)
is canonically equivalent to the abelian category of Nori motives
\mathbf{HM}(k)
. More precisely the functor (
2.5
) is an equivalence when
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}(k)

Proof.

(See also Proposition 4.12 of the paper
[
BVHP20
]
by Barbieri-Viale, Huber and Prest.)
Consider the triangulated functor
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{N},s}:\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{HM}(k))
constructed in Proposition 7.12 of the paper
[
CGAdS17
]
of Choudhury and Gallauer. Up to a natural isomorphism, the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Bti}_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{N},s}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{HM}(k))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forgetful
\textstyle{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{Q})}

is commutative. In particular, it provides a factorization of the cohomological functor
H^{0}\circ\mathrm{Bti}_{k}^{*}

\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(k)\xrightarrow{H^{0}\circ\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{N},s}}\mathbf{HM}(k)\xrightarrow{\textrm{forgetful}}\vec{(}\mathbf{Q}).

This implies the existence of a canonical faithful exact functor
\mathscr{M}(k)\rightarrow\mathbf{HM}(k)
such that

\textstyle{{\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{p}H^{0}_{\mathscr{M}}}
\scriptstyle{H^{0}\circ\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{N},s}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{M}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{HM}(k)}

is commutative up to a natural isomorphism. Using the universal properties, it is easy to see that it is a quasi-inverse to (
2.5
).
∎

The following conjecture seems reasonable and reachable via our current technology.

Conjecture 2.12
.

Let
X
be a smooth
k
-variety. Let
\mathscr{N}(X)
be the category of perverse motives constructed in
[
Ivo17
]
and

\mathsf{RL}^{\mathscr{N}}_{X}:\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{N}(X))

be the triangulated functor constructed in
[
Ivo16
]
. Then, the Betti realization
\mathrm{Bti}_{X}^{*}
is isomorphic to the composition

\mathbf{DA}_{\mathsf{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{N}(X))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{forgetful}}\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{P}(X))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{real}}\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q}).

If
2.12
holds then the same proof as the one of
2.11
implies the following

Conjecture 2.13
.

Let
X
be a smooth
k
-variety. Then, the functor (
2.5
) is an equivalence.

3.
Unipotent nearby and vanishing cycles

In
[
Bei87a
]
, Beĭlinson has given an alternate construction of unipotent vanishing cycles functors for perverse sheaves and has used it to explain a gluing procedure for perverse sheaves (see
[
Bei87a
, Proposition 3.1]
). In this section, our main goal is to obtain similar results for perverse Nori motives. Later on, the vanishing cycles functors for perverse Nori motives will play a crucial role in the construction of the inverse image functor (see
4
).

Given the way the abelian categories of perverse Nori motives are constructed from the triangulated categories of étale motives, our first step is to carry out Beĭlinson’s constructions
for perverse sheaves within the categories of étale motives or analytic motives (the latter categories being equivalent to the classical unbounded derived categories of sheaves of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces on the associated analytic spaces). This is done in
3.2
and
3.4
. Our starting point is the logarithmic specialization system constructed by Ayoub in
[
Ayo07b
]
. However, by working in triangulated categories instead of abelian categories as Beĭlinson did, one has to face the classical functoriality issues, one of the major drawback of triangulated categories. To avoid these problems and ensure that all our constructions are functorial we will rely heavily on the fact that the triangulated categories of motives underlie a triangulated derivator.

Only then, using the compatibility with the Betti realization, will we be able to obtain in
3.5
the desired functors for perverse Nori motives.

3.1.
Reminder on derivators

Let us recall some features of triangulated (a.k.a. stable) derivators
\mathbb{D}
needed in the construction of the motivic unipotent vanishing cycles functor and the related exact triangles. For the general theory, originally introduced by Grothendieck
[
Gro91
]
, we refer to the works
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
by Ayoub,
[
CN08
]
by Cisinki–Neeman,
[
Gro11
,
Gro13
]
by Groth and
[
Mal12
]

by Maltsiniotis.

We will assume that our derivator
\mathbb{D}
is defined over all small categories. In our applications, the derivators considered will be of the form
\mathbb{D}:=\mathbf{DA}(S,-)
for some
k
-variety
S
. Given a functor
\rho:A\rightarrow B
, we denote by

\rho^{*}:\mathbb{D}(B)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(A),\quad\rho_{*}:\mathbb{D}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(B),\quad\rho_{\sharp}:\mathbb{D}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(B)

the structural functor and its right and left adjoint. Note that in the literature on derivators, the notation
\rho_{!}
is used instead of
\rho_{\sharp}
. We follow here the notation used in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
.

Notation:
We let
\mathbf{e}
be the punctual category reduced to one object and one morphism. Given a small category
A
, we denote by
p_{A}:A\rightarrow{\mathbf{e}}
the projection functor and, if
a
is an object in
A
, we denote by
a:{\mathbf{e}}\rightarrow A
the functor that maps the unique object of
\mathbf{e}
to
a
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\underline{\mathbf{n}}
be the category

n\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow 1\leftarrow 0.

If one thinks of functors in
\mathsf{Hom}(A^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{e}))
as diagrams, then an object in
\mathbb{D}(A)
can be thought as a “coherent diagram”. Indeed, every object
M
in
\mathbb{D}(A)
has an underlying diagram called its
A
-skeleton and defined to be the functor
A^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{e}})
which maps an object
a
in
A
to the object
a^{*}M
of
\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{e}})
. This construction gives the
A
-skeleton functor

\mathbb{D}(A)\rightarrow\mathsf{Hom}(A^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{e}))

which is not an equivalence in general (coherent diagrams are richer than simple diagrams). We say that
M\in\mathbb{D}(A)
is a coherent lifting of a given diagram of shape
A
if its
A
-skeleton is isomorphic to the given diagram.

We will not give here the definition of a stable derivator (see e.g.
[
Ayo07a
, Definition 2.1.34]
or
[
Gro13
, Section 1 & Definition 4.1]
), but instead recall a few properties which will be constantly used.

(1)
Let
\rho:A\rightarrow B
be a functor and
b
be an object in
B
. Denote by
j_{A/b}:A/b\rightarrow A
and
j_{b\backslash A}:b\backslash A\rightarrow A
be the canonical functors where
A/b
and
b\backslash A
are respectively the slice and coslice categories. The exchange
2
-morphisms (given by adjunction)

b^{*}\rho_{*}\rightarrow(p_{A/b})_{*}j^{*}_{A/b}\quad;\quad(p_{b\backslash A})_{\sharp}j^{*}_{b\backslash A}\rightarrow b^{*}\rho_{\sharp}

are invertible (see
[
Ayo07a
, Définition 2.1.34]
or the base change axiom
Der 3
of
[
CN08
, Definition 1.11]
).

(2)
If a small category
A
admits an initial object
o
(resp. a final object
o
), then the
2
-morphism
o^{*}\rightarrow(p_{A})_{\sharp}
(resp. the
2
-morphism
(p_{A})_{*}\rightarrow o^{*}
) is invertible too (see
[
Ayo07a
, Corollaire 2.1.40]
).

(3)
Let
A
and
B
be small categories. Given an object
a\in A
we denote by
a:B\rightarrow A\times B
the functor which maps
b\in B
to the pair
(a,b)
. The
A
-skeleton of an object
M
in
\mathbb{D}(A\times B)
is defined to be the functor
A^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(B)
which maps an object
a
in
A
to the object
a^{*}M
of
\mathbb{D}(B)
. This construction gives the
A
-skeleton functor

\mathbb{D}(A\times B)\rightarrow\mathsf{Hom}(A^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbb{D}(B)).

This functor is conservative. Moreover if
A=\underline{\mathbf{1}}
, it is full and essentially surjective. (See the axioms
Der 2
and
Der 5
of
[
CN08
, Definition 1.11]
.)

We denote by
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}=\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}}
the category

(3.1)

\textstyle{{(1,1)}}
\textstyle{{(0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We denote by
\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}
the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
that does not contain the object
(0,0)
and by
i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}}:\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
the inclusion functor. We denote by
(-,1):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}
the fully faithful functor which maps
0
to
(0,1)
and
1
to
(1,1)
.
Similarly we denote by
\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
that does not contain the object
(1,1)
and
i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}}:\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
the inclusion functor. We denote by
(0,-):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(0,0)
and
(0,1)

An object
M
in
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxempty})
is said to be cocartesian (resp. cartesian) if and only if the canonical morphism
(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})^{*}M\rightarrow M
(resp.
M\rightarrow(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})^{*}M
) is an isomorphism. Since
\mathbb{D}
is stable, a square
M
in
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxempty})
is cartesian if and only if it is cocartesian.

Let
\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
be the category

(3.2)

\textstyle{{(2,1)}}
\textstyle{{(1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

There are three natural ways to embed
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
in
\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
and an object
M\in\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxbar})
is said to be cocartesian if the squares in
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxempty})
obtained by pullback along those embeddings are cocartesian.
A coherent triangle is a cocartesian object
M\in\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxbar})
such that
(0,1)^{*}M
and
(2,0)^{*}M
are zero. For such an object, we have a canonical isomorphism
(0,0)^{*}M\simeq(2,1)^{*}M[1]
and the induced sequence

(3.3)

(2,1)^{*}M\rightarrow(1,1)^{*}M\rightarrow(1,0)^{*}M\rightarrow(2,1)^{*}M[1]

is an exact triangle in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{e})
.

One of the main advantages of working in a stable derivator is the possibility to associate with a coherent morphism
M\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{1}})
functorially a coherent triangle. Let us briefly recall the construction of this triangle. Let
U
be the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
that does not contain
(0,0)
and
(1,0)
. Denote by
v:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow U
the functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(1,1)
and
(2,1)
and by
u:U\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
the inclusion functor. The image under the functor

u_{\sharp}v_{*}:\mathbb{D}(A\times\underline{\mathbf{1}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(A\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar})

of a coherent morphism
M
in
\mathbb{D}(A\times\underline{\mathbf{1}})
is a coherent triangle.
Using the properties
(1–2)
recalled above, we see that (
3.3
) provides an exact triangle

(3.4)

1^{*}M\rightarrow 0^{*}M\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(M)\rightarrow 1^{*}M[1]

where the cofiber functor
\mathsf{Cof}
is defined by

(3.5)

\mathsf{Cof}:=(1,0)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}:\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{1}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}({\mathbf{e}}).

Using the properties
(1–3)
recalled above, it is easy to see that this functor is also given by

\mathsf{Cof}=(0,0)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}.

In the exact triangle (
3.4
), the canonical morphism
0^{*}M\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(M)
is the
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton of the coherent morphism
(1,-)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}M
where
(1,-):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
is the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(1,0)
and
(1,1)
. Note that we have an isomorphism of functors

(1,-)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}M\simeq(0,-)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}:\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{1}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{1}}).

Similarly the boundary morphism
\mathsf{Cof}(M)\rightarrow 1^{*}M[1]
is the
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton of the coherent morphism
(-,0)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}M
where
(-,0):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
is the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(0,0)
and
(1,0)
.

The construction of the cofiber functor
\mathsf{Cof}
and the cofiber triangle (
3.4
) can be dualized to get a fiber functor
\mathsf{Fib}
and a fiber triangle. Let us recall the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\boxempty})
. Then, we have a morphism of exact triangles

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Fib}((-,1)^{*}M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{(1,1)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Fib}((-,0)^{*}M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}

which is functorial in
M
. Furthermore, if
M
is cartesian if and only if the canonical morphism

u:\mathsf{Fib}((-,1)^{*}M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Fib}((-,0)^{*}M)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the fact that we have functorially defined fibers, as we just recalled. The second statement is Proposition 15.1.10 of Moritz Groth’s
unpublished book
Introduction to the theory of derivators
.
Let us recall its proof. We have a commutative cube

\textstyle{{(1,0)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Fib}((-,0)^{*}M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{{(1,1)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1)^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Fib}((-,1)^{*}M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The front and back squares are cartesian by definition of an exact triangle. By
[
Gro13
, Proposition 4.6]
, the top square is cartesian if and only if the bottom square is
cartesian. But the top square is
M
and the bottom square is cartesian if and only if
u
is an isomorphism, hence the result.

∎

There is also a functorial version of the octahedron axiom in
\mathbb{D}
(see e.g.
[
Gro11
, Proof of Theorem 9.44]
or
[
Gro13
, Proof of Theorem 4.15]
), that is, there is a functor
\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{2}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(O)

which associates to a coherent sequence of morphisms a coherent octahedron diagram. Here the category
O\subseteq\underline{\mathbf{4}}\times\underline{\mathbf{2}}
is the full subcategory that does not contain the objects
(4,0)
and
(0,2)
. In other words,
O
is the category

\textstyle{{(4,2)}}
\textstyle{{(3,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(4,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(3,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(3,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Let
W
be the full subcategory of
O
that does not contain the objects
(1,1)
,
(2,1)
,
(3,1)
,
(0,0)
,
(1,0)
and
(2,0)
. Denote by
\omega:\underline{\mathbf{2}}\rightarrow W
the fully faithful functor which maps
0
,
1
and
2
respectively on
(2,2)
,
(3,2)
and
(4,2)
and by
w:W\rightarrow O
the inclusion functor. The octahedron diagram functor is defined to be the functor

w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}:\mathbb{D}(\underline{\mathbf{2}})\rightarrow\mathbb{D}(O).

Denote by
\mathsf{sm}:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{2}}
the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
0
and
1
and by
\mathsf{fm}:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{2}}
the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
1
and
2
. Denote also by
\mathsf{cm}:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{2}}
the functor which maps
0
and
1
respectively to
0
and
2
. Consider the fully faithful functor
\mathsf{fsq}:\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow O
which maps the square (
3.1
) to the square

\textstyle{{(4,2)}}
\textstyle{{(3,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(4,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(3,1).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Similarly we denote by
\mathsf{ssq}:\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow O
(resp.
\mathsf{csq}:\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow O
) the fully faithful functor which maps the square (
3.1
) to the square

\textstyle{{(3,2)}}
\textstyle{{(2,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(3,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(resp.
\textstyle{{(4,2)}}
\textstyle{{(2,2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(4,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,1)\textrm{).}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have canonical isomorphisms

\mathsf{fsq}^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}\simeq(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}\mathsf{fm}^{*},\quad\mathsf{ssq}^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}\simeq(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}\mathsf{sm}^{*}

and

\mathsf{csq}^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}\simeq(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}\mathsf{cm}^{*}.

Proof.

Let
i:\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}\rightarrow W
be the fully faithful functor that maps
(0,1)
,
(1,0)
and
(1,1)
respectively to
(3,2)
,
(4,1)
and
(4,2)
.
Since
\omega\circ\mathsf{fm}=i\circ(-,1)
, we get a natural transformation
i^{*}\omega_{*}\rightarrow(0,1)_{*}\mathsf{fm}^{*}
. Using the properties
(Der1–3)
, it is easy to see that this natural transformation is invertible. Similarly, since
w\circ i=\mathsf{fsq}\circ i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}}
, there is a natural transformation
(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}i^{*}\rightarrow\mathsf{fsq}^{*}w_{\sharp}
. Again, using the properties
(Der1–3)
, we see that it is invertible. This provides invertible natural transformations

\textstyle{{(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}i^{*}\omega_{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(i_{\boldsymbol{\ulcorner}})_{\sharp}(-,1)_{*}\mathsf{fm}^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{fsq}^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}.}}

The other invertible natural transformations are constructed similarly.
∎

In particular, it follows from
3.2
that
(3,1)^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Cof}\circ\mathsf{fm}^{*}
,
(2,0)^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Cof}\circ\mathsf{sm}^{*}
and
(2,1)^{*}w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Cof}\circ\mathsf{cm}^{*}
.
Since the inverse image of
w_{\sharp}\omega_{*}
along the fully faithful functor
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow O
that maps the square (
3.1
) to the square

\textstyle{{(3,1)}}
\textstyle{{(2,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(3,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

is a cocartesian square, by
3.2
and
[
Ayo07a
, Définition 2.1.34]
, we get a natural exact triangle

(3.6)

\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{fm}^{*}(-))\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{cm}^{*}(-))\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{sm}^{*}(-))\xrightarrow{+1}.

Let us recall
[
Ayo07a
, Lemma 1.4.8]
. Note that the functors
j^{*}:\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(U,\matheusm I)
and
j_{*}:\mathbf{DA}(U,\matheusm I)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)
used below are induced by the functoriality of the categories of presheaves on diagrams of schemes (see
[
Ayo07b
, §4.5]
for details).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\matheusm I
be a small category and
j:U\hookrightarrow X
be an open immersion.
Assume that we have a exact triangle

M\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}M\rightarrow C(M)\xrightarrow{+1}

for every given object
M\in\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)
. Then, for every morphism
\alpha:M\rightarrow N
in
\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)
there exists one and only one morphism
C(M)\rightarrow C(N)
such that the square

\textstyle{{C(M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C(\alpha)}
\textstyle{{M[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha[1]}
\textstyle{{C(N)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N[1]}

is commutative. Moreover the whole diagram

\textstyle{{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{j_{*}j^{*}M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}j^{*}\alpha}
\textstyle{{C(M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C(\alpha)}
\textstyle{{M[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha[1]}
\textstyle{{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}j^{*}N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C(N)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N[1]}

is commutative.

Note that in loc.cit. the lemma is stated only in the case
\matheusm I=\mathbf{e}
. However its proof works in the more general situation considered here.

We will need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\matheusm I
be a small category and
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism of separated
k
-schemes of finite type.
There exists a functor
\Delta^{*}_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\matheusm I)
such that, for every
M\in\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)
, the
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton of
\Delta^{*}_{f}(M)
is
M\rightarrow f_{*}f^{*}M
.

Proof.

Consider the diagram of
k
-varieties
(\mathscr{F},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\matheusm I):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\matheusm I\rightarrow(\mathrm{Sch}/k)
that maps
(0,i)
to
Y
and
(1,i)
to
X
and the canonical morphisms of diagrams of
k
-varieties

\textstyle{{(\mathscr{F},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\matheusm I)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\matheusm I)}
\textstyle{{(X,\matheusm I).}}

The functor
\Delta_{f}^{*}:=\beta_{*}\alpha^{*}
satisfies the desired property.

∎

Remark 3.5
.

Assume
\matheusm I=\mathbf{e}
. Given
M
in
\mathbf{DA}(X)
, we have an exact triangle

M\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}M\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\Delta^{*}_{j}(M))\xrightarrow{+1}

functorial in
M
. It follows from
[
Ayo07a
, Lemme 1.4.8]
that the functor
i_{!}i^{!}(-)[1]
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Cof}\circ\Delta^{*}_{j}(-)

Similarly we will need the following lemma. Its proof is completely similar to the one of
3.4
and will be omitted.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\matheusm I
be a small category and
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a smooth morphism of separated
k
-schemes of finite type.
There exists a functor
\Delta^{!}_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I\times\underline{\mathbf{1}})
such that for every
A\in\mathbf{DA}(X,\matheusm I)
the
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton of
\Delta^{!}_{f}(A)
is
f_{\sharp}f^{*}A\rightarrow A
.

Remark 3.7
.

Assume
\matheusm I=\mathbf{e}
. Given
M
in
\mathbf{DA}(X)
, as in
3.5
, we have an exact triangle

j_{!}j^{!}M\rightarrow M\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\Delta^{!}_{j}(M))\xrightarrow{+1}

functorial in
M
. It follows from (the dual statement of)
[
Ayo07a
, Lemme 1.4.8]
, that the functor
i_{*}i^{*}(-)
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Cof}\circ\Delta^{!}_{j}(-)
.

3.2.
Motivic unipotent vanishing cycles functor

Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
be a morphism of
k
-varieties. We consider the following diagram of
k
-varieties

\textstyle{{X_{\eta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{X_{\sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{G}_{m,k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}}}
\textstyle{{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}

where
i
denotes the zero section of
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
and
j
the open immersion of the complement. We denote also by
i
the closed immersion of the special fiber
X_{\sigma}
in
X
and by
j
the open immersion of the generic fiber
X_{\eta}
in
X
. Let
\mathsf{Log}_{f}
be the logarithmic specialization system constructed in
[
Ayo07b
, 3.6]
(see also
[
Ayo14a
, p.103–109]
). It is defined by

\mathsf{Log}_{f}:=\chi_{f}((-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})=:i^{*}j_{*}((-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})

where
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
is the commutative associative unitary algebra in
\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{G}_{m,k})
constructed in
[
Ayo07b
, Définition 3.6.29]
(see also
[
Ayo14a
, Définition 11.6]
). The monodromy triangle

(3.7)

\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}\xrightarrow{N}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)\xrightarrow{+1}

(see
[
Ayo07b
, Corollaire 3.6.21]
or
[
Ayo14a
, (116)]
) in the triangulated category
\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{G}_{m,k})

induces an exact triangle

\chi_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}_{f}(-)(-1)\xrightarrow{+1}.

To construct the motivic unipotent vanishing cycles functor, we shall use the fact that the

\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton functor

\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k},\underline{\mathbf{1}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Hom}(\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}))

is full and essentially surjective. This allows to choose an object
\mathscr{L}
in
\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k},\underline{\mathbf{1}})
that lifts the morphism
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
obtained as the composition of the adjunction morphism
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow j_{*}\mathbf{Q}(0)
and the image under
j_{*}
of the unit
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
of the commutative associative unitary algebra
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
. Moreover, using the monodromy triangle (
3.7
), we can fix an isomorphism between
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)
and the cofiber of
j^{*}\mathscr{L}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{Q}(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Q}(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Cof}(j^{*}\mathscr{L})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}

is commutative.

Consider the object
\mathscr{Q}:=\Delta^{*}_{j}(\mathscr{L})
in
\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k},\boldsymbol{\boxempty})
obtained by applying the functor
\Delta^{*}_{j}
of
3.4
. Its
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
-skeleton is the commutative square

\textstyle{{\mathbf{Q}(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}\mathbf{Q}(0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}.}

Let
\boldsymbol{\llcorner}
be the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
that does not contain
(0,1)
. Denote by
i_{\boldsymbol{\llcorner}}:\boldsymbol{\llcorner}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
the inclusion and by
p_{\boldsymbol{\boxempty},\boldsymbol{\llcorner}}:\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\llcorner}
the unique functor which is the identity on
\boldsymbol{\llcorner}
and maps
(0,1)
to
(0,0)
. Consider the functor

\Theta_{f}(-):=(p_{\boldsymbol{\boxempty},\boldsymbol{\llcorner}})^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\llcorner}})^{*}\Delta^{*}_{j}((p_{\underline{\mathbf{1}}})^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}\mathscr{L}):\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\boldsymbol{\boxempty}).

By construction,
\Theta_{f}(-)
is a coherent lifting of the commutative square

\textstyle{{\mathrm{Id}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}j^{*}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}).}

By pulling back along the closed immersion
i:X_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X
we get the functor

i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-):\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\boxempty})

which is a coherent lifting of the commutative square

\textstyle{{i^{*}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-)).}

Let
(-,1):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
be the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
. In particular, the
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
-skeleton of
(-,1)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
is the morphism

i^{*}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-)).

Definition 3.8
.

The motivic unipotent vanishing cycles functor
\Phi_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma})
is defined as the composition of
(-,1)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
and the cofiber functor:

\Phi_{f}:=\mathsf{Cof}\circ(-,1)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-).

By construction, we get
a natural transformation
can:\mathsf{Log}_{f}(-)\circ j^{*}\rightarrow\Phi_{f}(-)
and an exact triangle

(3.8)

i^{*}\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}_{f}(-)\circ j^{*}\xrightarrow{can}\Phi_{f}\xrightarrow{+1}.

We also get a natural transformation

var:\Phi_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)

such that
var\circ can=N
. Indeed, let
(-,0):\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
be the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
(0,0)
and
(1,0)
.

The chosen isomorphism between
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)
and the cofiber of
j^{*}\mathscr{L}
induces an isomorphism between
\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)
and the cofiber of
(-,0)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
such that the diagram

\textstyle{{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Cof}((-,0)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}

is commutative. On the other hand, the canonical morphism
(-,1)^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)\rightarrow(-,0)^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
in
\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}})
induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Cof}((-,0)^{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{{i^{*}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{can}
\textstyle{{\Phi_{f}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
.

By applying the coherent triangle functor
u_{\sharp}v_{*}
to the object
i^{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
of the category
\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\boxempty})=\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}})
, we get a functor

\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar})

which is a coherent lifting of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))[1].}
\textstyle{{i^{*}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{can}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\Phi_{f}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{var}
\textstyle{{i^{*}(-)[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The category
\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
is given by

\textstyle{{(1,2,1)}}
\textstyle{{(1,1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,2,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and we consider the functor
\mathsf{sq}:\boldsymbol{\boxempty}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
which maps (
3.1
) to the square

\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}}
\textstyle{(1,0,0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

inside
\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
. In the next subsection, we will be mainly focusing on the functor

\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\boldsymbol{\boxempty})

which is a coherent lifting of the commutative square

\textstyle{{\Phi_{f}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{var}
\textstyle{{i^{*}(-)[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\chi_{f}(j^{*}(-))[1].}

Remark 3.9
.

The square
\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}i^{*}\Theta_{f}
is cartesian. This can be deduced from the basic properties of cartesian squares (for example from
[
Gro13
, Proposition 4.6]
).

3.3.
Maximal extension functor

Let us now construct Beĭlinson’s maximal extension functor
\Xi_{f}
(see
[
Bei87a
]
) and the related exact triangles in the triangulated categories of étale motives. This will be essential to prove
3.15
and for gluing perverse motives. By applying the coherent triangle functor
u_{\sharp}v_{*}
to the object
\Theta_{f}(-)
in
\mathbf{DA}(X,\boldsymbol{\boxempty})=\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}})
, we get a functor

u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar})

which is a coherent lifting of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{j_{*}j^{*}(-))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1].}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Id}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(Here
\bullet
is some motive which we do not need to specify).
The category
\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\boxbar}
is given by

\textstyle{{(1,2,1)}}
\textstyle{{(1,1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(1,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,2,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,2,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Let
\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
be the full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\boxempty}
that does not contain
(1,1)
.
Then
\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
is the category

(3.9)

\textstyle{{(1,0,1)}}
\textstyle{{(1,1,0)}}
\textstyle{{(1,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We denote by
\alpha:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\boxbar}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}}
the functor which maps (
3.9
) to

\textstyle{(1,0,1)}
\textstyle{{(1,0,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,1)}}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,1,0)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{(0,0,0).}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Then,
\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-):\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner})
is a coherent lifting of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]}

and
(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)=(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
. Let
\beta:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
be the fully faithful functor defined by

\displaystyle(0,0,0)\mapsto(0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle(1,0,0)\mapsto(0,1,0,0)

\displaystyle(0,0,1)\mapsto(0,0,0,1)

\displaystyle(1,0,1)\mapsto(0,1,0,1)

\displaystyle(0,1,0)\mapsto(0,0,1,0)

\displaystyle(1,1,0)\mapsto(1,1,1,0).

The
\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
-skeleton of the functor

\Sigma_{f}(-):=\beta^{*}\circ\Delta^{!}_{j}\circ\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-):\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner})

is now the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{!}\big(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}\big)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{j_{*}\big(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)\big)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]}

where the non-zero diagonal morphism
is obtained via the canonical morphism
j_{!}\rightarrow j_{*}
and the monodromy operator. Note that we have

(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\Sigma_{f}=(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)=(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-).

In particular, we have canonical isomorphisms
(0,0,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)=j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]
and

(0,0,1)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)=\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\Xi_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X)
be the functor defined by

\Xi_{f}(-):=(1,0)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-)).

We also define
\Omega_{f}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X)
to be the functor

\Omega_{f}(-):=(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-)).

By construction, we have an exact triangle

(3.10)

\Omega_{f}(-)\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\oplus(0,1)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))\rightarrow(0,0)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))\xrightarrow{+1}.

Since the canonical morphisms

\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]=(0,0,1)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)\rightarrow(0,1)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))

and

j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]=(0,0,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)\rightarrow(0,0)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))

are isomorphisms, the exact triangle (
3.10
) can be rewritten as

\Omega_{f}(-)\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\oplus\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]\xrightarrow{+1}.

On the other hand, we have an exact triangle

(1,1,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)\rightarrow(0,1,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\xrightarrow{+1}

that is an exact triangle

(3.11)

j_{!}\big(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}\big)\rightarrow j_{*}\big(j^{*}(-)\otimes f_{\eta}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}(-1)\big)\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Proposition 3.11
.

There are exact triangles

(3.12)

i_{*}\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))\rightarrow\Xi_{f}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1]\xrightarrow{+1}

and

(3.13)

j_{!}j^{*}(-)[1]\rightarrow\Xi_{f}\rightarrow i_{*}\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))(-1)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Proof.

Let us first construct (
3.12
) using the functorial version of the octahedron axiom (see
3.1
). Recall that by definition

\Xi_{f}(-):=(1,0)^{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))=\mathsf{Cof}((-,1,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)).

Let us set

\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-):=\Delta^{!}_{j}\circ\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-):\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner})

so that
\Sigma_{f}(-)=\beta^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-)
. Now let
\gamma:\underline{\mathbf{2}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\underline{\mathbf{1}}\times\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}
be the fully faithful functor that maps
0
,
1
and
2
respectively to
(0,0,1,0)
,
(0,1,1,0)
and
(1,1,1,0)
. Recall that
\mathsf{cm}:\underline{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbf{2}}
is the fully faithful functor that maps
0
and
1
respectively to
0
and
2
. Then,
\beta\circ(-,1,0)=\gamma\circ\mathsf{cm}
. In particular, we get that

(-,1,0)^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)=\mathsf{cm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-).

Using the exact triangle (
3.6
) given by the functorial octahedron axiom, we get an exact triangle

\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{fm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-))\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{sm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-))\xrightarrow{+1}.

However, by construction, we have an exact triangle

j_{!}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})\rightarrow j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee})\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{fm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-))\xrightarrow{+1}.

Using
3.7
, we see that

\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{fm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-))
is isomorphic to

i_{*}\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-)):=i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}(j^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}_{\eta}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}).

On the other hand,
\mathsf{sm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-)=(0,1,-)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)
,
so that we get an isomorphism

\mathsf{Cof}(\mathsf{fm}^{*}\gamma^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}_{f}(-))=(0,0,0)^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)=j_{*}j^{*}(-)[1].

This constructs the exact triangle (
3.12
).
Consider now the localization triangle

j_{!}j^{*}\Xi_{f}(-)\rightarrow\Xi_{f}(-)\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}\Xi_{f}(-)\xrightarrow{+1}.

To obtain (
3.13
) it is enough to check that
j^{*}\Xi_{f}(-)
is isomorphic to
j^{*}(-)[1]
and that
i^{*}\Xi_{f}(-)
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))
. The first isomorphism is obtained by applying
j^{*}
to (
3.12
) and the second isomorphism is obtained by applying
i^{*}
to (
3.11
).

∎

Proposition 3.12
.

There are exact triangles

(3.14)

i_{*}\mathsf{Log}_{f}(j^{*}(-))\rightarrow\Omega_{f}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}(-)[1]\xrightarrow{+1}

and

(3.15)

j_{!}j^{*}(-)[1]\rightarrow\Omega_{f}\rightarrow i_{*}\Phi_{f}(-)\xrightarrow{+1}.

Proof.

Using (
3.12
), the exact triangle (
3.14
) is obtained by applying
3.1
to the cartesian square
(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}\mathsf{Cof}(\Sigma_{f}(-))
.

Since
j^{*}i_{*}=0
, (
3.14
) provides an isomorphism between
j^{*}\Omega_{f}(-)
and
j^{*}(-)[1]
. Now, consider the localization triangle

j_{!}j^{*}\Omega_{f}\rightarrow\Omega_{f}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}\Omega_{f}(-)\xrightarrow{+1}.

To construct (
3.15
), it is enough to obtain an isomorphism between
i^{*}\Omega_{f}(-)
and
\Phi_{f}(-)
.
By definition

i^{*}\Omega_{f}(-)=(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}\mathsf{Cof}(i^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)).

However since
i^{*}j_{!}=0
, the canonical morphism

(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}i^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathsf{Cof}(i^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-))

is an isomorphism.
Given that

(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\Sigma_{f}=(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\alpha^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)=(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-),

we get isomorphisms

\textstyle{{(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}(0\times\mathrm{Id}_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}i^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}\mathsf{Cof}(i^{*}\Sigma_{f}(-))=i^{*}\Omega_{f}(-)}
\textstyle{{(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-).}}

By
3.9
, the canonical morphism

\Phi_{f}(-)=(1,1)^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)\rightarrow(1,1)^{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})_{*}(i_{\boldsymbol{\lrcorner}})^{*}\mathsf{sq}^{*}u_{\sharp}v_{*}\Theta_{f}(-)

is an isomorphism. This concludes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Betti realization

Let
X
be a complex algebraic variety. Let
\mathbf{AnDA}(X)
be the triangulated category of analytic motives. This category is obtained as the special case of the category
\mathbf{SH}^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathfrak{M}}(X)
considered in
[
Ayo10
]
when the stable model category
\mathfrak{M}
is taken to be the category of unbounded complexes of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces with its projective model structure. Recall that the canonical triangulated functor

(3.16)

i^{*}_{X}:\mathbf{D}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{AnDA}(X)

is an equivalence of categories (see
[
Ayo10
, Théorème 1.8]
). Here
\mathbf{D}(X)
denotes the (unbounded) derived category of sheaves of
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces on the associated analytic space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. The functor

\mathsf{An}_{X}:(\mathrm{Sm}/X)\rightarrow(\mathrm{AnSm}/X^{\mathrm{an}})

which maps a smooth
X
-scheme
Y
to the associated
X^{\mathrm{an}}
-analytic space
Y^{\mathrm{an}}
induces a triangulated functor

(3.17)

\mathsf{An}^{*}_{X}:\mathbf{DA}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{AnDA}(X).

The Betti realization
\mathrm{Bti}^{*}_{X}
of
[
Ayo10
]
is obtained as the composition of (
3.17
) and a quasi-inverse to (
3.16
).

Let
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}_{\mathscr{P}}
be the image under the Betti realization of the motive
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
and consider the specialization system it defines

\mathsf{Log}_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-):=i^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}j^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}((-)\otimes(f_{\eta})_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}_{\mathscr{P}}):\mathbf{D}(X_{\eta})\rightarrow\mathbf{D}(X_{\sigma}).

Recall that in
3.2
we fixed an object
\mathscr{L}
in
\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k},\underline{\mathbf{1}})
that lifts the morphism
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
obtained as the composition of the adjunction morphism
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow j_{*}\mathbf{Q}(0)
and the image under
j_{*}
of the unit
\mathbf{Q}(0)\rightarrow{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
of the commutative associative unitary algebra
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}
.

Let
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{P}}
the image in
\mathbf{D}(X,\underline{\mathbf{1}})
of
\mathscr{L}
. Using this object, we can perform the same constructions as in
3.2
and
3.3
using the derivator
\mathbf{D}(X,-)
to obtain functors

\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-),\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-):\mathbf{D}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{D}(X)

and

\Phi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-):\mathbf{D}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{D}(X_{\sigma})

and four exact triangles: the two triangles

i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}j_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\rightarrow\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}j_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}[1]\xrightarrow{+1},\;j^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}j^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[1]\rightarrow\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-1)\xrightarrow{+1}

and the two triangles

i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}j_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\rightarrow\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}[1]\xrightarrow{+1},\;j^{\mathscr{P}}_{!}j^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}[1]\rightarrow\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}\Phi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\xrightarrow{+1}.

Moreover, we have canonical natural transformations

\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\circ\mathsf{Log}_{f}\rightarrow\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*},\;\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\circ\Phi_{f}\rightarrow\Phi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}

and

\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\circ\Xi_{f}\rightarrow\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*},\;\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\circ\Omega_{f}\rightarrow\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\circ\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\;

which are isomorphisms when applied to constructible motives (see
[
Ayo10
, Théorème 3.9]
) and are also compatible with the various exact triangles.

As proved in
[
Ayo10
, Théorème 4.9]
, the Betti realization is compatible with the (total) nearby cycles functors for constructible motives. In this subsection, we will need the compatibility of the Betti realization with the unipotent nearby cycles functors.

Lemma 3.13
.

The functor
\mathsf{Log}_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-)
is isomorphic to the unipotent nearby cycles functor
\psi^{\mathrm{un}}_{f}(-)
.

Let
e:\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times};z\mapsto\exp(z)

be the universal cover of the punctured complex plane
\mathbf{C}^{\times}
.
The group of deck transformations is identified with
\mathbf{Z}
by mapping the integer
k\in\mathbf{Z}
to the deck transformation
z\mapsto z+2i\pi k
.

Let
\mathscr{E}_{n}
be the unipotent rational local system on
\mathbf{C}^{\times}
of rank
n+1
with (nilpotent) monodromy given by one Jordan block of maximal size. It underlies a variation of
\mathbf{Q}
-mixed Hodge structures described e.g. in §1.1 of Morihiko Saito’s
[
Sai90a
]
.

Let us recall the description of this local system and relate it to Ayoub’s logarithmic motive
{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}_{n}
. The following description is given in M. Saito,
[
Sai89
, 2.3. Remark]
. Let
\mathscr{E}_{n}
be the subsheaf of
e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}
annihilated by
(T-\mathrm{Id})^{n+1}
where
T
is the automorphism of
e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}
induced by the deck transformation corresponding to
1\in\mathbf{Z}
. The restriction of
T
to
\mathscr{E}_{n}
is unipotent and we denote by
N=\log T
the associated nilpotent endomorphism.

The sheaf
\mathscr{E}_{n}
is a local system on
\mathbf{C}^{\times}
of rank
n+1
. Let
(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{1}
be its fiber over
1
. We have an inclusion

(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{1}\subseteq(e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}})_{1}=\prod_{k\in\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}})_{2i\pi k}=\prod_{k\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{Q}.

Note that the automorphism
T
acts by mapping a sequence
(a_{k})_{k\in\mathbf{Z}}
to
(a_{k+1})_{k\in\mathbf{Z}}
.
Let
\tau_{n}
be the element in
(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{1}
given by
\tau_{n}=(k^{n}/n!)_{k\in\mathbf{Z}}
. The family
(1,\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
is a basis of
(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{1}
such that

T(\tau_{r})=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\tau_{k}/(r-k)!

for every
r\in[\kern-0.96002pt[1,n]\kern-0.96002pt]
. The matrix with respect to the basis
(1,\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
of the unipotent endomorphism
T
of
(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{1}
is thus given by
\sum_{k=0}^{n}(J_{n})^{k}/k!

where
J_{n}
is the nilpotent Jordan block of size
n+1
and therefore
N
is given by the Jordan block
J_{n}
in the basis
(1,\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
.

The multiplication
e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow e_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{C}}
induces a morphism of local systems
\mathscr{E}_{k}\otimes\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{k+\ell}
. In particular, for
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have a canonical morphism
\mathscr{E}_{1}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{n}
which defines a morphism

(3.18)

\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathscr{E}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{n}.

If
\tau:=\tau_{1}
, then
\tau_{n}=\tau^{n}/n!
and the above description of
\mathscr{E}_{n}
implies that (
3.18
) is an isomorphism.

Let us consider the Kummer natural transform

e_{K}:\mathrm{Id}(-)(-1)[-1]\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}(-)

in Betti cohomology (see
[
Ayo07b
, Définition 3.6.22]
).
By 5.1 Lemma in M. Saito’s
[
Sai06
]
, the local system
\mathscr{E}_{1}
fits into an exact triangle

\mathbf{Q}(-1)[-1]\xrightarrow{e_{K}}\mathbf{Q}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{1}\xrightarrow{+1}.

By
[
Ayo10
, Théorème 3.19]
the Betti realization is compatible with the Kummer transform (for constructible motives). In particular, we have a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\mathscr{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{1}
where
\mathscr{K}\in\mathbf{DA}(\mathbf{G}_{m})
is the motivic Kummer extension, that is, the cone of the Kummer natural transform for étale motives (see
[
Ayo07b
, Lemme 3.6.28]
). Since the Betti realization
\mathrm{Bti}^{*}
is a symmetric monoidal functor, it induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{Bti}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}_{n}=\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathscr{K}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathrm{Bti}^{*}\mathscr{K}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathscr{E}_{1}\xrightarrow{\eqref{eq:MorLocSys}}\mathscr{E}_{n}

for every integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, we get an isomorphism

(3.19)

{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}_{\mathscr{P}}:=\mathrm{Bti}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}og^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{E}

where
\mathscr{E}
is the ind-local system given by
\mathscr{E}=\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\times}}\mathscr{E}_{n}
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\mathbf{Q})
, the unipotent nearby cycles functor
\psi_{f}^{\mathrm{un}}
is given by

\psi^{\mathrm{un}}_{f}(K)=i^{*}_{\mathscr{P}}j^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}(K\otimes(f_{\eta})_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}\mathscr{E})

(see
[
Sai89
, (2.3.3)]
, Beilison’s
[
Bei87a
]
or Reich’s
[
Rei10
]
). With this description,
3.13
is an immediate consequence of (
3.19
).

Corollary 3.14
.

The functors

{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-):=\mathsf{Log}_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-)[-1],\quad{}^{p}\Phi_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-):=\Phi_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}(-)[-1],\quad{}^{p}\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-):=\Xi^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-)[-1]

and

\quad{}^{p}\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-):=\Omega^{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(-)[-1]

are
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structure.

Proof.

Since the functor
\psi_{f}^{\mathrm{un}}(-)[-1]
is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structure, the corollary is an immediate consequence of
3.13
and the exact triangles relating the various functors.
∎

3.5.
Application to perverse motives

Now, we can apply the universal property of the categories of perverse motives to obtain four exact functors

{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}(-):\mathscr{M}(X_{\eta})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma}),\;{}^{p}\Phi_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}(-):\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma})

and

{}^{p}\Xi_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}(-):\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X),\;{}^{p}\Omega_{f}^{\mathscr{M}}(-):\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X).

Moreover we have four canonical exact sequences obtained from the exact triangles relating the four functors used in the construction. Two exact sequences

0\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}(-))\rightarrow{}^{p}\Xi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)\rightarrow 0

and

0\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)\rightarrow{}^{p}\Xi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)(-1)\rightarrow 0.

As well as two exact sequences

(3.20)

0\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}(-))\rightarrow{}^{p}\Omega^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}(-)\rightarrow 0

and

0\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}(-)\rightarrow{}^{p}\Omega^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\Phi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow 0.

These four functors and the associated exact sequences are compatible with the various functors and exact triangles constructed in
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4
.

Now we can prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion of
k
-varieties. Then, the functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X)

is fully faithful and its essential image is the kernel, denoted by
\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)
, of the exact functor

j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}:\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(U)

where
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is the open immersion of the complement of
Z
in
X
.

We first consider the case of the immersion of a special fiber.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety and
f:X\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
be a morphism. Let
i:X_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X
be the closed immersion of the special fiber in
X
and
Z
be a closed subscheme of
X_{\sigma}
. Then, the exact functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X_{\sigma})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)

is an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

We may assume
Z=X_{\sigma}
. Indeed, let
u:X\setminus Z\hookrightarrow X
and
v:X_{\sigma}\setminus Z\hookrightarrow X_{\sigma}
be the open immersion. By
2.3
applied to cartesian square

\textstyle{{X_{\sigma}\setminus Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{{X\setminus Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{{X_{\sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X,}

we get an isomorphism
u^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}i_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}\simeq i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}v^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
. Since the functor
i^{\prime\mathscr{M}}_{*}
is conservative (it is faithful exact), we see that an object
A
in
\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma})
belongs to
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits v^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
if and only if
i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}A
belongs to
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits u^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
. Hence, it is enough to show that

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{X_{\sigma}}(X)

is an equivalence.

Let us show that the functor
{}^{p}\Phi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}
is a quasi-inverse. Let
X_{\eta}
be the generic fiber and
j:X_{\eta}\hookrightarrow X
be the open immersion. The exact triangle (
3.8
), provides an isomorphism of endomorphisms of
\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma})
between
i^{*}i_{*}
and
\Phi_{f}[-1]i_{*}
. By composing with the isomorphism of functors
i^{*}i_{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}
, we get an isomorphism of functors between the identity of
\mathbf{DA}(X_{\sigma})
and
{}^{p}\Phi_{f}[-1]i_{*}
.

Similarly, we get an isomorphism between the identity of
\mathrm{D}(X_{\sigma},\mathbf{Q})
and the functor
\Phi_{f}^{\mathscr{P}}[-1]i^{\mathscr{P}}_{*}
. Since these isomorphisms are compatible with the Betti realization, the property
P2
, ensures that
{}^{p}\Phi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}
is isomorphic to the identity functor of the category
\mathscr{M}(X_{\sigma})
.

An isomorphism between the identity of
\mathscr{M}_{X_{\sigma}}(X):=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
and
i^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}{}^{p}\Phi_{f}
is provided by the exact sequences

0\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-))\rightarrow{}^{p}\Omega^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow\mathrm{Id}(-)\rightarrow 0

and

0\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}(-)\rightarrow{}^{p}\Omega^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}{}^{p}\Phi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}(-)\rightarrow 0

(the first terms vanish for objects in the kernel of
j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
3.15
.

Using
2.7
, we may assume that
X
is an affine scheme. Let
U
be the open complement of
Z
in
X
and let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
be elements in
\mathscr{O}(X)
such that
U=D(f_{1})\cup\cdots D(f_{r})
. Let
Z_{r+1}=X
and set
Z_{k}=Z_{k+1}\setminus D(f_{k})
for
k\in[\kern-0.96002pt[1,r]\kern-0.96002pt]
. Let
i_{k}:Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{k+1}
be the closed immersion. We have
Z_{1}=Z
and
i=i_{r}\circ i_{r-1}\circ\dots\circ i_{1}
, so that the functor
i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)
is obtained as the composition

\mathscr{M}(Z)\xrightarrow{(i_{1})_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{M}_{Z}(Z_{2})\xrightarrow{(i_{2})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\mathscr{M}_{Z}(Z_{3})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{Z}(Z_{k})\xrightarrow{(i_{k})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}}\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X).

By
3.16
, all these functors are equivalences. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Inverse images

The purpose of this section is to extend the (contravariant)
2
-functor
{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
constructed in
2.5
into a (contravariant)
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}:(\mathrm{Sch}/k)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{TR}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}.

To do this, we first use the vanishing cycles functor to show that the (covariant)
2
-functor
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}
admits a global left adjoint
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
(we recall that a global left adjoint is unique up to unique isomorphism and refer to
[
Ayo07a
, Définition 1.1.18]
for the definition). Then, we show that the
2
-functors
{}^{\mathrm{Liss}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
and
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
can be glued into a
2
-functor
\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
.

4.1.
Inverse image by a closed immersion

By
[
Ayo07a
, Proposition 1.1.17]
, to show that
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}
admits a global left adjoint
{}^{\mathrm{Imm}}\mathsf{H}^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}
it suffices to show that for every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
the functor
i_{*}^{\mathscr{M}}
admits a left adjoint; this in turn is proved in

4.2
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion. Then, the functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))

is fully faithful and its essential image is is the kernel, denoted by
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
, of the exact functor

j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(U))

where
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is the open immersion of the complement of
Z
in
X
.

Proof.

We know that the
essential image of
i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
is contained
in
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
by
3.15
. We now want to prove that
the functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
is an equivalence of categories.
Note that the obvious
t
-structure on
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
induces a
t
-structure
on
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
, whose heart is the thick abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)

of
\mathscr{M}(X)
.
By
3.15
,
the functor
i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X)
induces an equivalence of categories

\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)
. So, by
[
Bei87c
, Lemma 1.4]
, the functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
is an equivalence of categories if and only
if, for any
A,B
in
\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)
and

i\geqslant 1
, and any class

u\in\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(X)}(A,B)
, there exists a monomorphism
B\hookrightarrow B^{\prime}

in
\mathscr{M}_{Z}(X)
such that the image of
u
in
\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(X)}(A,B^{\prime})
is
0
.

Suppose that
j:V\hookrightarrow X
is an affine open immersion, that
A
is
an object of
\mathscr{M}(X)
and that
B
is an object of
\mathscr{M}(V)
.
Let
i\geqslant 1
. Then, we have

\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(X)}(A,j^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}B)=\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(V)}(j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,B)

by
2.5
, and, if
u\in\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(V)}(j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,B)
and

B\hookrightarrow B^{\prime}
is a monomorphism of
\mathscr{M}(V)
such that
the image of
u
in
\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(V)}(j^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}A,B^{\prime})
is
0
, then, the image
in
\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(X)}(A,j^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}B^{\prime})
of the element of
\Ext^{i}_{\mathscr{M}(X)}(A,j^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}B)

corresponding to
u
is also
0
. Applying this to an open cover

j_{1}:U_{1}\hookrightarrow X
, …,
j_{n}:U_{n}\hookrightarrow X
of
X
by
affine subsets and using the fact the canonical map

B\rightarrow\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}(j_{r})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(j_{r})^{*}_{\mathscr{M}}B
given by
2.5

is a monomorphism for every
object
B
of
\mathscr{M}(X)
, we reduce to the case where
X
is affine.

If
X
is affine, then, as in the proof of
3.15
,
we write
i=i_{r}\circ\dots\circ i_{1}
, where
Z_{1}=Z
,
Z_{r+1}=X
, and, for
every
k\in\{1,\dots,r\}
,

i_{k}:Z_{k}\rightarrow Z_{k+1}
is the immersion of the complement of an open
set of the form
D(f)
, with
f\in\mathscr{O}(Z_{k+1})
. It suffices to show
that each
(i_{k})^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z_{k}))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z_{k}}(\mathscr{M}(Z_{k+1}))
is an
equivalence of categories. So we may assume that there exists

f\in\mathscr{O}(X)
such that
i
is the immersion of the complement
of
D(f)
. In that case, we showed in the proof of
3.16

that the trivial derived functor of the exact functor

{}^{p}\Phi^{\mathscr{M}}_{f}:\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)
induces a quasi-inverse of

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion. Then, the functor

i^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))

admits a left adjoint.

Proof.

By
4.1
, it suffices to show that the inclusion functor

(4.1)

\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))

admits a left adjoint
C^{\bullet}
. Let
j:U\hookrightarrow X
be the open immersion of the complement of
Z
in
X
. Let us first assume that
U
is affine. In that case, given
A
in
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
, we define
C^{\bullet}(A)
as the mapping cone of the canonical morphism
j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A\rightarrow A
given by
2.5
. This construction induces a triangulated functor
C^{\bullet}:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
and there is a canonical exact triangle

j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A\rightarrow A\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(A)\rightarrow j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A[1]
,
which shows that
C^{\bullet}
takes its values in the full subcategory
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
. Let
B\in\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(X))
. Using the long exact sequence associated with this triangle and
2.5
which ensures that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}(j^{\mathscr{M}}_{!}j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A,B[n])=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(U))}(j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}A,j_{\mathscr{M}}^{*}B[n])=0,

we get a functorial isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}(C^{\bullet}(A),B)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))}(A,B)

as desired.

In the general case, the adjoint
C^{\bullet}
can be constructed by considering a finite set
I
and an affine open covering
\mathscr{U}=(j_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow U)_{i\in I}
. For every
J\subseteq I
, let
j_{J}
be the inclusion

\bigcap_{i\in J}U_{i}\hookrightarrow X
.
We define an exact functor
C^{\bullet}:\mathscr{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathscr{M}(X))
